\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{F(z)-F(z_{0})}{z-z_{0}}-G(z_{0})\Big\|_{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|z-z_{0}|}{2\pi}\int_{\gamma}\|F(w)\|_{\mathcal{I}}\frac{1}{|w-z|\cdot|w-z_{0}|^{2}}\,|dw|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{w\in\gamma}\|F(w)\|_{\mathcal{I}}\cdot\frac{|z-z_{0}|}{2\pi}\int_{\gamma}\frac{1}{|w-z|\cdot|w-z_{0}|^{2}}\,|dw|.

Since
f
is continuous, the right hand side tends to
0
as
z\to z_{0}
. Hence,
G=F^{\prime}
and so
F
is holomorphic.
∎

Lemma 5.4.5
.

Let
0\leq A\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty}.
The function
z\to A^{z}
is
\mathcal{L}_{\infty}-
valued holomorphic on the half-plane
\{z\;:\;\Re(z)>0\}.

Proof.

For
z_{0},z\in\mathbb{C}
with
\Re(z_{0})>0
and
\Re(z)>0,
we define the operator
A^{z_{0}}\log(A)
by means of functional calculus (the convention
0^{z_{0}}\log(0)=0
is used).

Hence,

\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{A^{z}-A^{z_{0}}}{z-z_{0}}-A^{z_{0}}\log(A)\Big\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{0\leq\lambda\leq\|A\|_{\infty}}\Big|\frac{\lambda^{z}-\lambda^{z_{0}}}{z-z_{0}}-\lambda^{z_{0}}\log(\lambda)\Big|

\displaystyle=O(z-z_{0}),\quad z_{0}\to z.

Hence, for all
z
with
\Re(z)>0

A^{z}-A^{z_{0}}=A^{z_{0}}\log(A)(z-z_{0})+o(1),\quad z_{0}\to z

and so
A^{z}
is
\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
-valued holomorphic with derivative
A^{z}\log(A)
.
∎

Lemma 5.4.6
.

Let
p>2
and assume
A
and
B
satisfy condition
5.4.1
. The mapping

z\to[B,A^{z}],\quad\Re(z)>1,

is a holomorphic
\mathcal{L}_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}-
valued function.

Proof.

We take care to note that since
p>2
, the ideal
\mathcal{L}_{p/2,\infty}
can be equipped with a norm generating the same topology as
that of the canonical quasi-norm (see
[
3
, Chapter 4, Lemma 4.5]
). Denote such a norm as
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{L}_{p/2,\infty}}^{\prime}
.

Denote

F(z)=[B,A^{z}].

Since
F(z)=BA^{z}-A^{z}B
it now follows from Lemma
5.4.5
that
F
is
\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
-valued holomorphic.
Due to Lemma
5.4.4
, it now suffices to show that
F
is
\mathcal{L}_{p/2,\infty}
-valued
and
\mathcal{L}_{p/2,\infty}
-continuous.

Let
\phi
be a compactly supported smooth function on
\mathbb{R}
, such that
0\leq\phi\leq 1
and
\phi=1
on the interval

[0,\|A\|_{\infty}]
. Define

\phi_{z}(t)=|t|^{z}\phi(t),\quad t\in\mathbb{R},\Re(z)>1.

Since
\phi=1
on the spectrum of
A
, we have that
\phi_{z}(A)=A^{z}
and so for all
z,z_{1},z_{2}
with
\Re(z),\Re(z_{1}),\Re(z_{2})>1
,

\displaystyle F(z)

\displaystyle=[B,\phi_{z}(A)]

\displaystyle F(z_{1})-F(z_{2})

\displaystyle=[B,(\phi_{z_{1}}-\phi_{z_{2}})(A)].

Now we refer to
[
50
]
, where it is proved that if
A
and
B
are self-adjoint operators and
r>1
is such that
[A,B]\in\mathcal{L}_{r,\infty}
,
then for all Lipschitz functions
f
, there is a constant
c_{r}
such that:

\|[f(A),B]\|_{\mathcal{L}_{r,\infty}}\leq c_{r}\|f^{\prime}\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\|[A,B]\|_{\mathcal{L}_{r,\infty}}.

Since
p>2
, we may apply this result with
r=p/2
and since
\phi
is smooth and compactly supported,
we may take
f=\phi_{z}
. Thus,

\|F(z)\|_{\mathcal{L}_{p/2,\infty}}^{\prime}\leq c_{p/2}\|\phi_{z}^{\prime}\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\|[B,A]\|_{\mathcal{L}_{p/2,\infty}}^{\prime}

and similarly taking
f=\phi_{z_{1}}-\phi_{z_{2}}
,

\|F(z_{1})-F(z_{2})\|_{\mathcal{L}_{p/2,\infty}}^{\prime}\leq c_{p/2}\|\phi_{z_{1}}^{\prime}-\phi_{z_{2}}^{\prime}\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\|[B,A]\|_{\mathcal{L}_{p/2,\infty}}^{\prime}.

Note that for
z_{1}\to z_{2}
we have:

\|\phi_{z_{1}}^{\prime}-\phi_{z_{2}}^{\prime}\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\to 0

and hence
F
is
\mathcal{L}_{p/2,\infty}
-valued continuous. The result now follows.
∎

Lemma 5.4.7
.

Let
p>2
and let
A
and
B
satisfy Condition
5.4.1
. Then:

(i)

The mapping
F_{0}(z):=B^{z-1}[B,A^{z-\frac{1}{2}}]A^{\frac{1}{2}}+[B,A^{\frac{1}{2}}]A^{\frac{1}{2}}Y^{z-1}
is an
\mathcal{L}_{1}-
valued
holomorphic function for the domain
\Re(z)>p-1.

(ii)

The mapping
F_{1}(z):=B^{z-1}A^{z-1}
is an
\mathcal{L}_{\frac{p}{p-2},1}-
valued holomorphic function for the domain
\Re(z)>p-1.

(iii)

The mapping
F_{2}(z):=B^{z-1}[BA,A^{z-1}]
is an
\mathcal{L}_{1}-
valued holomorphic function for the domain
\Re(z)>p-1.

(iv)

The mapping
F_{3}(z):=Y^{z-1}
is an
\mathcal{L}_{\frac{p}{p-2},1}-
valued holomorphic function for the domain
\Re(z)>p-1.
(Recall that
Y=A^{1/2}BA^{1/2}
).

Proof.

We first prove (
ii
). Fix
q\in(p,p+2).
If
\Re(z)>q-1,
then

B^{z-1}A^{z-1}=B^{z-q+1}B^{q-2}A\cdot A^{z-2}.

By Lemma
5.4.3
, we have that

B^{q-2}A\in\mathcal{L}_{\frac{p}{q-2},\infty}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\frac{p}{p-2},1}

and correspondingly by Lemma
5.4.5
the mapping
z\mapsto B^{z-1}A^{z-1}
is continuous in the
\mathcal{L}_{p/(p-2),1}
norm.

Moreover Lemma
5.4.5
implies that the mappings
z\to B^{z-q+1}
and
z\to A^{z-2}
are
\mathcal{L}_{\infty}-
valued holomorphic for
\Re(z)>q-1.

Thus by Lemma
5.4.4
the mapping
z\to B^{z-1}A^{z-1}
is
\mathcal{L}_{\frac{p}{p-2},1}-
valued holomorphic for
\Re(z)>q-1.

Since
q>p
is arbitrary, (
ii
) follows.

Now we prove (
iii
). Again fix
q\in(p,p+2)
and let
z
satisfy
\Re(z)>q-1.
We rewrite
F_{2}
as:

\displaystyle F_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=B^{z}A^{z}-B^{z-1}A^{z-1}BA

\displaystyle=B^{z-1}A^{z-1}\cdot[A,B]+[B,B^{z-1}A^{z}]

(5.10)

\displaystyle=B^{z-1}A^{z-1}\cdot[A,B]+B^{z-1}\cdot[B,A]\cdot A^{z-1}+B^{z-1}A\cdot[B,A^{z-1}].

The first summand in (
5.10
) can be written as

B^{z-q+1}\cdot B^{q-2}A\cdot A^{z-2}\cdot[A,B].

Due to Lemma
5.4.5
, the mappings
z\to B^{z-q+1}
and
z\to A^{z-2}
are
\mathcal{L}_{\infty}-
valued holomorphic for
\Re(z)>q-1.
By Lemma
5.4.3
, we have that

B^{q-2}A\in\mathcal{L}_{\frac{p}{q-2},\infty}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\frac{p}{p-2},1}.

The element
[A,B]=A^{1/2}[A^{1/2},B]+[A^{1/2},B]A^{1/2}
belongs to
\mathcal{L}_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}.
Hence, the first summand is
\mathcal{L}_{1}
-valued holomorphic for
\Re(z)>q-1.

The second summand in (
5.10
) can be written as

z\to B^{z-q+1}\cdot B^{q-2}[B,A]\cdot A^{z-1}.

By our assumption of Condition
5.4.1
, the operator
B^{q-2}[B,A]
belongs to
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and accordingly the map
z\to B^{z-1}\cdot[B,A]\cdot A^{z-1}
is
\mathcal{L}_{1}
-continuous. Once again due to Lemma
5.4.5
, the mappings
z\to B^{z-q+1}
and
z\to A^{z-1}
are
\mathcal{L}_{\infty}-
valued holomorphic for
\Re(z)>q-1.
Hence, the second summand of (
5.10
) is
\mathcal{L}_{1}
-valued holomorphic for
\Re(z)>q-1.

We now treat the third summand of (
5.10
).
By Lemma
5.4.6
, the mapping

z\to[B,A^{z-1}],\quad\Re(z)>q-1,

is a holomorphic
\mathcal{L}_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}-
valued function. Due to Condition
5.4.1
, we have that

B^{q-2}A\in\mathcal{L}_{\frac{p}{q-2},\infty}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\frac{p}{p-2},1}.

Hence

z\to B^{z-q+1}\cdot B^{q-2}A\cdot[B,A^{z-1}],\quad\Re(z)>q-1,

is an
\mathcal{L}_{1}
-valued holomorphic function.

So, all three summands in (
5.10
) are holomorphic for
\Re(z)>q-1.
Thus,
z\to F_{2}(z)
is holomorphic for
\Re(z)>q-1.
Since
q>p
is arbitrary, it follows that
z\to F_{2}(z)
is holomorphic for
\Re(z)>p-1.
This proves (
iii
).

For (
iv
), we note that this is an immediate consequence of the assumption of Condition
5.4.1
.(
iii
)
and Lemma
5.4.5
.

Finally we prove (
i
). We write
F_{0}(z)
as:

\displaystyle F_{0}(z)

\displaystyle=B^{z-1}[B,A^{z-1}A^{1/2}]A^{1/2}+[B,A^{1/2}]A^{1/2}Y^{z-1}

\displaystyle=F_{2}(z)+B^{z-1}A^{z-1}[B,A^{1/2}]A^{1/2}+[B,A^{1/2}]A^{1/2}F_{3}(z)

\displaystyle=F_{2}(z)+F_{1}(z)[B,A^{1/2}]A^{1/2}+[B,A^{1/2}]F_{3}(z).

Hence, by (
ii
), (
iii
) and (
iv
) and Condition
5.4.1
.(
iv
),

F_{0}(z)\in\mathcal{L}_{1}+\mathcal{L}_{p/(p-2),1}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{p/2,\infty}+\mathcal{L}_{p/2,\infty}\mathcal{L}_{p/(p-2),\infty}.

so by the Hölder-type inequality (
2.7
),
F_{0}(z)\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
, and it continuous in the
\mathcal{L}_{1}
-norm. So by Lemma
5.4.4
,
F_{0}
is
\mathcal{L}_{1}
-valued holomorphic.
∎

Lemma 5.4.8
.

Let
s\to H(s)
be a bounded
\mathcal{L}_{\infty}-
valued function measurable in the weak operator topology (see Definition
2.3.1
). Let
g_{z}
be as in Theorem
5.2.1
. Define

G(z):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}H(s)\widehat{g}_{z}(s)ds,\quad\Re(z)>1

as a weak operator topology integral.

(i)

G
is an
\mathcal{L}_{\infty}-
valued holomorphic function for the domain
\Re(z)>1.

(ii)

if there is
r>1
such that for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
we have
\|H(s)\|_{r,\infty}\leq 1+|s|
, then
G
is an
\mathcal{L}_{r,\infty}-
valued holomorphic function
for the domain
\Re(z)>1.

Proof.

Define:

g_{1,z}=\frac{\partial}{\partial z}g_{z},\quad z\in\mathbb{C},\quad\Re(z)>1.

Define the
\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
-valued function
G_{1}
by:

G_{1}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}H(s)\hat{g}_{1,z}(s)ds.

We will show that
G
is
\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
-valued holomorphic by showing that
G_{1}
is the derivative of
G
.

Let
z,z_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
have real part greater than
1
. Then we have

\frac{G(z)-G(z_{0})}{z-z_{0}}-G_{1}(z_{0})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}H(s)\Big(\frac{\hat{g}_{z}(s)-\hat{g}_{z_{0}}(s)}{z-z_{0}}-\hat{g}_{1,z_{0}}(s)\Big)ds.

So by the triangle inequality,

\Big\|\frac{G(z)-G(z_{0})}{z-z_{0}}-G^{\prime}(z_{0})\Big\|_{\infty}\leq\esssup_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\|H(s)\|_{\mathcal{L}_{\infty}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big|\frac{\hat{g}_{z}(s)-\hat{g}_{z_{0}}(s)}{z-z_{0}}-\hat{g}_{1,z_{0}}(s)\Big|ds.

By
[
51
, Lemma 7]
, we have an absolute constant
c_{\mathrm{abs}}
such that:

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big|\frac{\hat{g}_{z}(s)-\hat{g}_{z_{0}}(s)}{z-z_{0}}-\hat{g}_{1,z_{0}}(s)\Big|ds\leq c_{abs}\Big(\Big\|\frac{g_{z}-g_{z_{0}}}{z-z_{0}}-g_{1,z_{0}}\Big\|_{2}+\Big\|\frac{g_{z}^{\prime}-g_{z_{0}}^{\prime}}{z-z_{0}}-g_{1,z_{0}}^{\prime}\Big\|_{2}\Big).

The assertion (
i
) follows now from Theorem
5.3.2
, and hence moreover we have that
G_{1}=G^{\prime}
.

Let us now establish (
ii
). Indeed, by Lemma
2.3.3
, we have

\displaystyle\|G(z)\|_{r,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{r}{r-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+|s|)|\hat{g}_{z}(s)|ds,

\displaystyle\|G^{\prime}(z)\|_{r,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{r}{r-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+|s|)|\hat{g}_{1,z}(s)|ds,

\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{G(z)-G(z_{0})}{z-z_{0}}-G^{\prime}(z_{0})\Big\|_{r,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{r}{r-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+|s|)\Big|\frac{\hat{g}_{z}(s)-\hat{g}_{z_{0}}(s)}{z-z_{0}}-\hat{g}_{1,z_{0}}(s)\Big|ds.

Once more using
[
51
, Lemma 7]
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+|s|)|\hat{g}_{z}(s)|ds=\|\hat{g}_{z}\|_{1}+\|\hat{g^{\prime}}_{z}\|_{1}\leq c_{\mathrm{abs}}\|g_{z}\|_{W^{2,2}}.

Similarly,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+|s|)|\hat{g}_{1,z}(s)|ds=\|\hat{g}_{1,z}\|_{1}+\|\hat{g^{\prime}}_{1,z}\|_{1}\leq c_{abs}\|g_{1,z}\|_{W^{2,2}}

and

\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+|s|)\Big|\frac{\hat{g}_{z}(s)-\hat{g}_{z_{0}}(s)}{z-z_{0}}-\hat{g}_{1,z_{0}}(s)\Big|ds=

=\Big\|\frac{\hat{g}_{z}-\hat{g}_{z_{0}}}{z-z_{0}}-\hat{g}_{1,z_{0}}\Big\|_{1}+\Big\|\frac{\hat{g^{\prime}}_{z}-\hat{g^{\prime}}_{z_{0}}}{z-z_{0}}-\hat{g^{\prime}}_{1,z_{0}}\Big\|_{1}\leq

\leq c_{abs}\Big\|\frac{g_{z}-g_{z_{0}}}{z-z_{0}}-g_{1,z_{0}}\Big\|_{W^{2,2}}.

We now deduce (
ii
) from Theorem
5.3.2
.
∎

Lemma 5.4.9
.

Let
p>2
and let
A
and
B
satisfy Condition
5.4.1
. If
X\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty},
then

(i)

the mapping

G_{1}(z):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]Y^{-is}XB^{is}\hat{g}_{z}(s)ds,

is an
\mathcal{L}_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}-
valued holomorphic function for
\Re(z)>1.

(ii)

the mapping

G_{2}(z):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}A^{is}Y^{-is}XB^{is}\hat{g}_{z}(s)ds,

is
\mathcal{L}_{\infty}-
valued holomorphic for
\Re(z)>1.

(iii)

the mapping

G_{3}(z):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}Y^{-is}XB^{is}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]\hat{g}_{z}(s)ds,

is
\mathcal{L}_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}-
valued holomorphic for
\Re(z)>1.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.4.8
(
i
), the functions
G_{1},

G_{2}
and
G_{3}
are
\mathcal{L}_{\infty}-
valued holomorphic.
In particular, this proves (
ii
). We now prove the first and third assertions.

Set

H_{1}(s)=[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]Y^{-is}XB^{is},\quad s\in\mathbb{R},

and

H_{2}(s)=Y^{-is}XB^{is}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}],\quad s\in\mathbb{R}.

For every
s\in\mathbb{R},
we have for
j=1,2
,

\Big\|H_{j}(s)\Big\|_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}\leq\|X\|_{\infty}\cdot\Big\|[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]\Big\|_{\frac{p}{2},\infty},

Let
\phi_{s}(t):=|t|^{1+2is},

t\in\mathbb{R}.
We now write

[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]=[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},\phi_{s}(A^{\frac{1}{2}})].

We again refer to
[
50
]
. Since
p>2
and
\phi_{s}
is a Lipschitz function, we have

\displaystyle\|[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]\|_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}

\displaystyle\leq c_{p}\|\phi_{s}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\|[BA,A^{\frac{1}{2}}]\|_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}

\displaystyle\leq c_{p}(1+|s|)\|[BA,A^{\frac{1}{2}}]\|_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}.

Hence for
j=1,2
we have:

\|H_{j}(s)\|_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}\leq c_{p}(1+|s|)\|[BA,A^{\frac{1}{2}}]\|_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}\|X\|_{\infty}.

The assertions (
i
) and (
iii
) now follow from Lemma
5.4.8
.(
ii
)
upon taking
j=1
for (
i
) and
j=2
for (
iii
).
∎

Lemma 5.4.10
.

Let
p>2
and let
A
and
B
satisfy Condition
5.4.1
. Let
T_{z}(s)
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
be defined as in Theorem
5.2.1
. Then if
\Re(z)>p-1
we have:

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\|T_{z}(s)\|_{1}\cdot|\hat{g}_{z}(s)|ds<\infty.

Proof.

We recall the definition of
T_{z}(s)
:

T_{z}(s)=B^{z-1+is}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{z-\frac{1}{2}+is}]Y^{-is}+B^{is}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]Y^{z-1-is},\quad s\in\mathbb{R}.

Consider the first summand in the definition of
T_{z}(s)
. Using the Leibniz rule:

\displaystyle B^{z-1+is}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{z-\frac{1}{2}+is}]Y^{-is}

\displaystyle=B^{z-1+is}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{z-1}A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]Y^{-is}

\displaystyle=B^{is}B^{z-1}A^{z-1}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]Y^{-is}

\displaystyle\quad+B^{is}B^{z-1}[BA,A^{z-1}]A^{is}Y^{-is}.

By the
\mathcal{L}_{1}
-triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|B^{z-1+is}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle,A^{z-\frac{1}{2}+is}]Y^{-is}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|B^{z-1}A^{z-1}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]\|_{1}+\|B^{z-1}[BA,A^{z-1}]\|_{1}.

We now apply the Hölder-type inequality (
2.7
),

\displaystyle\|B^{z-1+is}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle,A^{z-\frac{1}{2}+is}]Y^{-is}\|_{1}

(5.11)

\displaystyle\leq\|B^{z-1}A^{z-1}\|_{\frac{p}{p-2},1}\|[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]\|_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}+\|B^{z-1}[BA,A^{z-1}]\|_{1}.

Consider the function
\phi_{s}(t)=|t|^{1+2is}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Immediately,
\phi_{s}
is Lipschitz and
\|\phi_{s}^{\prime}\|_{L_{\infty}}\leq 2(1+|s|).
Since
p>2
, we may apply the result of
[
50
]
to obtain:

\|[BA^{1/2},\phi(A^{1/2})]\|_{p/2,\infty}\leq C_{p}(1+|s|)\|[BA^{1/2},A^{1/2}]\|_{p/2,\infty}.

Therefore,

(5.12)

\|[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]\|_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}\leq C_{p}(1+|s|)\|[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}}]\|_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}.

Combining (
5.11
) and (
5.12
), we have

\displaystyle\|B^{z-1+is}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle,A^{z-\frac{1}{2}+is}]Y^{-is}\|_{1}

(5.13)

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}(1+|s|)\|[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}}]\|_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}\|B^{z-1}A^{z-1}\|_{\frac{p}{p-2},1}+\|B^{z-1}[BA,A^{z-1}]\|_{1}.

Let us now consider the second summand in
T_{z}(s).
Using the Hölder inequality in the form of (
2.7
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|B^{is}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]Y^{z-1-is}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]Y^{z-1}\|_{1}

(5.14)

\displaystyle\leq\|[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]\|_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}\|Y^{z-1}\|_{\frac{p}{p-2},1}.

Now combining (
5.14
) with (
5.12
), we arrive at:

\displaystyle\|B^{is}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle,A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]Y^{z-1-is}\|_{1}

(5.15)

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}(1+|s|)\|[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}}]\|_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}\|Y^{z-1}\|_{\frac{p}{p-2},1}.

Now we may combine (
5.13
) and (
5.15
):

\displaystyle\|T_{z}(s)\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|B^{z-1}[BA,A^{z-1}]\|_{1}

\displaystyle\quad+c_{abs}(1+|s|)\|[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}}]\|_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}\cdot\Big(\|B^{z-1}A^{z-1}\|_{\frac{p}{p-2},1}+\|Y^{z-1}\|_{\frac{p}{p-2},1}\Big).

By Lemma
5.4.7
,

\|B^{z-1}[BA,A^{z-1}]\|_{1},\|B^{z-1}A^{z-1}\|_{\frac{p}{p-2},1},\|Y^{z-1}\|_{\frac{p}{p-2},1}<\infty,\quad\Re(z)>p-1.

Hence,

\|T_{z}(s)\|_{1}\leq C_{A,B,z}\cdot(1+|s|).

We now have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\|T_{z}(s)\|_{1}\cdot|\hat{g}_{z}(s)|ds\leq C_{A,B,z}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+|s|)|\hat{g}_{z}(s)|ds.

By
[
51
, Lemma 7]
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+|s|)|\hat{g}_{z}(s)|ds=\|g_{z}\|_{2}+\|g_{z}^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq c_{abs}\|g_{z}\|_{W^{2,2}}.

Thus,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\|T_{z}(s)\|_{1}|\hat{g}_{z}(s)|ds\leq C_{A,B,z}\|g_{z}\|_{W^{2,2}}.

The assertion follows now from Theorem
5.3.2
.
∎

Corollary 5.4.11
.

If
p>2
and
A
and
B
satisfy condition
5.4.1
, then for all
\Re(z)>p-1
we have:

B^{z}A^{z}-(A^{1/2}BA^{1/2})^{z}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}.

Proof.

The integral formula from Lemma
5.2.1
is valid for
\Re(z)>1
. Since
p>2
, we therefore have a weak operator topology integral:

B^{z}A^{z}-(A^{1/2}BA^{1/2})^{z}=T_{z}(0)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}T_{z}(s)\widehat{g}_{z}(s)\,ds.

From Lemma
5.4.10
, we have that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\|T_{z}(s)\|_{1}\cdot|\hat{g}_{z}(s)|ds<\infty.

Thus by Lemma
2.3.2
, we have that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}T_{z}(s)\widehat{g}_{z}(s)\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
.

Recalling the definition of
T_{z}(0)
:

T_{z}(0):=B^{z-1}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{z-\frac{1}{2}}]+[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}}]Y^{z-1}

So by (
5.11
) and (
5.14
),
T_{z}(0)\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
. Therefore,
B^{z}A^{z}-(A^{1/2}BA^{1/2})^{z}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
.
∎

Lemma 5.4.12
.

Assume that
p>2
and let
A
and
B
satisfy Condition
5.4.1
. If
X\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty},
then for
\Re(z)>p
:

{\rm Tr}\Big(X\Big(B^{z}A^{z}-(A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}})^{z}\Big)\Big)={\rm Tr}(XF_{0}(z))-\sum_{k=1}^{3}{\rm Tr}(G_{k}(z)F_{k}(z))

Here, the functions
F_{k}
are as in Lemma
5.4.7
and the functions
G_{k}
are as in Lemma
5.4.9
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.4.10
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\|T_{z}(s)\|_{1}|\widehat{g}_{z}(s)|\,ds<\infty.

So by Lemma
2.3.2
, we have:

\int_{\mathbb{R}}T_{z}(s)\widehat{g}_{z}(s)ds\in\mathcal{L}_{1}

and

{\rm Tr}\Big(X\int_{\mathbb{R}}T_{z}(s)\widehat{g}_{z}(s)ds\Big)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\rm Tr}(XT_{z}(s))\widehat{g}_{z}(s)ds.

By Theorem
5.2.1
, we have:

(5.16)

{\rm Tr}\Big(X\Big(B^{z}A^{z}-(A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}})^{z}\Big)\Big)={\rm Tr}(XT_{z}(0))-\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\rm Tr}(XT_{z}(s))\hat{g}_{z}(s)ds

for
\Re(z)>p.

Observing that
F_{0}(z)=T_{z}(0)
, we have:

(5.17)

{\rm Tr}\Big(X\Big(B^{z}A^{z}-(A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}})^{z}\Big)\Big)={\rm Tr}(X\cdot F_{0}(z))-\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\rm Tr}(XT_{z}(s))\hat{g}_{z}(s)ds.

By (
5.11
) and (
5.14
), we have

\displaystyle B^{z-1}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{z-\frac{1}{2}+is}]

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{L}_{1},

\displaystyle[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]Y^{z-1}

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{L}_{1}.

Now by the definition of
T_{z}(s),
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(XT_{z}(s))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}(Y^{-is}XB^{is}B^{z-1}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{z-\frac{1}{2}+is}])

\displaystyle\quad+\mathrm{Tr}(Y^{-is}XB^{is}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]Y^{z-1}).

By an application of the Leibniz rule, we have

B^{z-1}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{z-\frac{1}{2}+is}]=B^{z-1}A^{z-1}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]+B^{z-1}[BA,A^{z-1}]A^{is}.

Each of the above terms is
\mathcal{L}_{1}
, by (
5.11
) and Lemma
5.4.7
.(
iii
) respectively.
Therefore,

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}(X\cdot T_{z}(s))

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}([BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]Y^{-is}XB^{is}B^{z-1}A^{z-1})

\displaystyle+{\rm Tr}(A^{is}Y^{-is}XB^{is}B^{z-1}[BA,A^{z-1}])+{\rm Tr}(Y^{-is}XB^{is}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]Y^{z-1}).

Thus,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\rm Tr}

\displaystyle(XT_{z}(s))\hat{g}_{z}(s)ds

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]Y^{-is}XB^{is}\hat{g}_{z}(s)ds\cdot B^{z-1}A^{z-1}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm Tr}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}A^{is}Y^{-is}XB^{is}\hat{g}_{z}(s)ds\cdot B^{z-1}[BA,A^{z-1}]\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm Tr}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}Y^{-is}XB^{is}[BA^{\frac{1}{2}},A^{\frac{1}{2}+is}]\hat{g}_{z}(s)ds\cdot Y^{z-1}\Big).

Using the notations from Lemma
5.4.7
and Lemma
5.4.9
, we may summarise the above equality as:

(5.18)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\rm Tr}(XT_{z}(s))\hat{g}_{z}(s)ds=\sum_{k=1}^{3}{\rm Tr}(G_{k}(z)F_{k}(z)).

Combining (
5.17
) and (
5.18
) completes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to complete the proof of Theorem
5.4.2
.

Proof of Theorem
5.4.2
.

We will show that the function

A(z):={\rm Tr}(X\cdot F_{0}(z))-\sum_{k=1}^{3}{\rm Tr}(G_{k}(z)F_{k}(z))

is analytic for
\Re(z)>p-1
.

For the
F_{0}
term, we use Lemma
5.4.7
.(
i
): the mapping
z\to XF_{0}(z)
is
\mathcal{L}_{1}-
valued analytic for
\Re(z)>p-1.

For the
G_{1}F_{1}
term, we use Lemma
5.4.7
.(
ii
) and Lemma
5.4.9
.(
i
) to see that the mapping
z\to G_{1}(z)F_{1}(z)
is
\mathcal{L}_{1}-
valued analytic for
\Re(z)>p-1.

For the
G_{2}F_{2}
term, we use Lemma
5.4.7
.(
iii
)
and Lemma
5.4.9
.(
ii
) to see that the mapping
z\to G_{2}(z)F_{2}(z)
is
\mathcal{L}_{1}-
valued analytic for
\Re(z)>p-1.

Finally, for the
G_{3}F_{3}
term, we use Lemma
5.4.7
.
(
iv
) and Lemma
5.4.9
.(
iii
) to see that mapping
z\to G_{3}(z)F_{3}(z)
is
\mathcal{L}_{1}-
valued analytic for
\Re(z)>p-1.

Hence,
A
is holomorphic in the set
\Re(z)>p-1
. By Lemma
5.4.12
,

A(z)={\rm Tr}\Big(X\Big(B^{z}A^{z}-(A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}})^{z}\Big)\Big)

and so the proof is complete.
∎

5.5.
Criterion for universal measurability in terms of a
\zeta-
function

In this section we provide a sufficient condition for universal measurability of operators in
\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}
.

We recall that a linear functional
\varphi
on the weak Schatten ideal
\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}
is called
a trace if for all unitary operators
U
and
T\in\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}
, we have
\varphi(U^{*}TU)=\varphi(T)
. Equivalently,
for all bounded operators
A
we have
\varphi(AT)=\varphi(TA)
. We say that
\varphi
is normalised if

\varphi\left(\mathrm{diag}\left\{\frac{1}{n+1}\right\}_{n\geq 0}\right)=1.

An operator
T\in\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}
is called
universally measurable
if all normalised traces take the same value on
T
.

In this section we prove Theorem
1.2.7
, which provides a sufficient condition for operators of the form
AV
,
A\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
,
V\in\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}

to be universally measurable. This result is new, and is sufficiently powerful to allow us to prove Theorem
1.2.5
. A similar characterisation is provided in
[
63
, Theorem 4.13]
, but the
result provided here is stronger. A previously known characterisation of universal measurability
in terms of a heat trace can be found in
[
15
, Proposition 6]
.

Let
b
be a signed Borel measure on
[0,\infty)
. Recall that
b
can be written as a difference of two positive measures,
b=b_{+}-b_{-}

such that
b_{+}
and
b_{-}
are mutually singular to each other. Given a Borel set
S
, the total variation of
b
on
S
is defined to be
\mathrm{Var}_{S}(b):=b_{+}(S)+b_{-}(S)
.

We will consider measures
b
which satisfy,

(5.19)

\sup_{x\geq 0}\mathrm{Var}_{[x,x+1]}(b)=c_{b}<\infty.

Clearly any measure of finite total variation will satisfy this condition, as will some measures with infinite total variation such as Lebesgue measure and the measure
d\nu(t)=\sin(t)dt
.

Lemma 5.5.1
.

Let
b
be a signed Borel measure satisfying the condition (
5.19
) and let
f
be the Laplace transform of
b
, that is,

f(z):=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-tz}\,db(t),\quad\Re(z)>0.

The function
f
is analytic on the half-plane
\{\Re(z)>0\}.

Proof.

For every
n\geq 0,
let

f_{n}(z)=\int_{0}^{n}e^{-tz}db(t),\quad z\in\mathbb{C}.

Each function
f_{n},

n\geq 0,
is entire. Let
\varepsilon>0.
For
\Re(z)>\varepsilon,
we have

|f(z)-f_{n}(z)|=|\int_{n}^{\infty}e^{-tz}db(t)|\leq\sum_{k\geq n}e^{-k\varepsilon}\mathrm{Var}_{[k,k+1]}(b)\leq c_{b}\frac{e^{-n\varepsilon}}{1-e^{-\varepsilon}}.

Therefore,
f_{n}\to f
uniformly on the half-plane
\{\Re(z)>\varepsilon\}.
Since
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary, it follows that
f_{n}\to f
uniformly on the compact subsets of the half-plane
\{\Re(z)>0\}.
Thus,
f
is analytic on the half-plane
\{\Re(z)>0\}.

∎

For
x\geq 0
, we denote

b(x):=b([0,x]).

We caution the reader that
b(x)
does not denote
b(\{x\})
nor the Radon-Nikodym derivative of
b
at
x
.

The following lemma is very similar to
[
61
, Lemma 2.1.3]
. However we require a slightly different formulation of that result and we were unable to find an English-language
version of its proof, and so we include a self-contained proof here.

Lemma 5.5.2
.

Let
b
be a signed Borel measure on
[0,\infty)
satisfying (
5.19
), and and let
f
be the Laplace transform of
b.

For
x\geq 1
,
x\to\infty
, we have

b(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{(1-t^{2})^{2}}{\frac{1}{x}+it}f(\frac{1}{x}+it)e^{(\frac{1}{x}+it)x}dt+O(1),\quad x\geq 1.

Proof.

Let
x\geq 1
. By definition we have:

\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{(1-t^{2})^{2}}{\frac{1}{x}+it}f(\frac{1}{x}+it)e^{(\frac{1}{x}+it)x}dt=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{(1-t^{2})^{2}}{\frac{1}{x}+it}e^{(\frac{1}{x}+it)(x-s)}db(s)dt.

Examining the integrand, we see that the function

(s,t)\mapsto\frac{(1-t^{2})^{2}}{x^{-1}+t}e^{(\frac{1}{x}+it)(x-s)}

is bounded above in absolute value by

(s,t)\mapsto exe^{-s/x}.

Since
x\geq 0
the function
s\to e^{-s/x}
is in
L_{1}([0,\infty),b)
we may interchange the integrals to get:

\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{(1-t^{2})^{2}}{\frac{1}{x}+it}f(\frac{1}{x}+it)e^{(\frac{1}{x}+it)x}\,dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{(1-t^{2})^{2}}{\frac{1}{x}+it}e^{(\frac{1}{x}+it)(x-s)}\,dt\right)\,db(s)

Now we refer to Proposition
A.5.1
, where it is proved that:

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{(1-t^{2})^{2}}{\frac{1}{x}+it}e^{(\frac{1}{x}+it)(x-s)}dt=(1+x^{-2})^{2}\chi_{[0,x]}(s)+\min\{1,(x-s)^{-2}\}\cdot O(1).

Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{(1-t^{2})^{2}}{\frac{1}{x}+it}f(\frac{1}{x}+it)e^{(\frac{1}{x}+it)x}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x}(1+x^{-2})^{2}db(s)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\infty}\min\{1,(x-s)^{-2}\}\cdot O(1)\,db(s).

We let
h(s)
be the total variation of
b
on
[0,s]
. That is,
h(s):=\mathrm{Var}_{[0,s]}(b)
. Then by the triangle inequality, there is a positive constant
c_{\mathrm{abs}}
such that

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{0}^{\infty}\min\{1,(x-s)^{-2}\}\cdot O(1)\cdot db(s)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq c_{abs}\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}\min\{1,(x-s)^{-2}\}dh(s)

\displaystyle=c_{abs}\cdot\sum_{k\geq 0}\int_{k}^{k+1}\min\{1,(x-s)^{-2}\}dh(s)

\displaystyle\leq c_{abs}\cdot\sum_{k\geq 0}\sup_{s\in[k,k+1]}\min\{1,(x-s)^{-2}\}\cdot\int_{k}^{k+1}dh(s)

\displaystyle\leq c_{abs}\cdot c_{b}\cdot\sum_{k\geq 0}\sup_{s\in[k,k+1]}\min\{1,(x-s)^{-2}\}

\displaystyle=O(1)

Thus,

(5.20)

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{(1-t^{2})^{2}}{\frac{1}{x}+it}f(\frac{1}{x}+it)e^{(\frac{1}{x}+it)x}dt=\int_{0}^{x}(1+x^{-2})^{2}db(s)+O(1).

By the definition of
b(x)
,

|b(x)-b(0)|\leq\mathrm{Var}_{[0,x]}(b)\leq c_{b}(1+x),\quad x>0.

Therefore,

\int_{0}^{x}(1+x^{-2})^{2}db(s)=(1+x^{-2})^{2}\cdot(b(x)-b(0))=b(x)+O(1),\quad x\geq 1.

A combination of the latter inequality with (
5.20
) completes the proof.
∎

The following Lemma is similar in spirit to (but much stronger than) the well-known Wiener-Ikehara Tauberian theorem
[
59
, Theorem 14.1]
.

Lemma 5.5.3
.

Let
b
be a signed Borel measure on
[0,\infty)
satisfying (
5.19
), and let
f

be the Laplace transform of
b.
If there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
f
has analytic continuation to a half-plane
\{z\;:\;\Re(z)>-\epsilon\},
then
for
x\geq 1

b(x)=O(1),\quad x\to\infty.

Proof.

We write
f(z)=f(0)+zf_{0}(z),
where
f_{0}
is an analytic function on the half-plane
\{z\;:\;\Re(z)>-\epsilon\}.
In particular, since the (closure of the) set

\Big\{\frac{1}{x}+it:\ x\geq 1,\ t\in[-1,1]\Big\},

is a compact subset in
\{\Re(z)>-\epsilon\},
it follows that

(5.21)

\sup_{x\geq 1}\sup_{t\in[-1,1]}|f_{0}(\frac{1}{x}+it)|<\infty.

The assertion of Lemma
5.5.2
is now written as follows.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}(1-t^{2})^{2}f_{0}(\frac{1}{x}+it)

\displaystyle e^{(\frac{1}{x}+it)x}dt+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{(1-t^{2})^{2}}{\frac{1}{x}+it}f(0)e^{(\frac{1}{x}+it)x}dt

(5.22)

\displaystyle=b(x)+O(1),\quad x\to\infty.

The first summand in the left hand side is bounded for
x\geq 1.
due to (
5.21
). By Proposition
A.5.1
, we have

(5.23)

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{(1-t^{2})^{2}}{\frac{1}{x}+it}e^{(\frac{1}{x}+it)x}dt=1+O(x^{-2}),\quad x\to\infty.

Combining (
5.22
) and (
5.23
), we get:

b(x)+O(1)=f(0)+O(1),\quad x\to\infty.

So
b(x)=O(1)
as
x\to\infty
.
∎

In order to continue our discussion of measurability we refer to the concept of a modulated operator. This
theory was introduced in
[
37
]
and is developed extensively in
[
45
, Section 11.2]
. If
V\in\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}
is positive,
and
T\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
, we say that
T
is
V
-modulated if

\sup_{t>0}t^{1/2}\|T(1+tV)^{-1}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}<\infty.

It can be easily seen that if
T
is
V
-modulated, and
A\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
, then
AT
is
V
-modulated (this is also
[
45
, Proposition 11.2.2]
).
It is proved in
[
45
, Lemma 11.2.8]
that
V
is
V
-modulated, and therefore that
AV
is
V
-modulated.

The relevance of the notion of a
V
-modulated operator to measurability comes from
[
45
, Theorem 11.2.3]
, which states
that if
V\geq 0
is in
\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}
,
\ker(V)=0
,
T
is
V
-modulated and
\{e_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
is an eigenbasis for
V
ordered
so that
Ve_{n}=\mu(n,V)e_{n}
for
n\geq 0
, then

(a)

T\in\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}
and
\mathrm{diag}\{\langle Te_{n},e_{n}\rangle\}_{n=0}^{\infty}\in\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}

(b)

We have:

\sum_{k=0}^{n}\lambda(k,T)-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\langle Te_{k},e_{k}\rangle=O(1),\quad n\to\infty.

Recall that
\{\lambda(k,T)\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
denotes an eigenvalue sequence for
T
, ordered with non-increasing absolute value.

Lemma 5.5.4
.

Let
0\leq V\in\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}
satisfy
\ker(V)=0
and let
A\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty}.
Let
\{e_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
be an eigenbasis for
V
ordered
such that
Ve_{k}=\mu(k,V)e_{k}
. We have

\sum_{k=0}^{n}\lambda(k,AV)=\sum_{\mu(k,V)>\frac{\|V\|_{1,\infty}}{n}}\langle Ae_{k},e_{k}\rangle\mu(k,V)+O(1).

Here,
e_{k}
is the eigenvector of
V
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\mu(k,V).

Proof.

We have that
AV
is
V-
modulated.
[
45
, Theorem 11.2.3]
now states that as
n\to\infty

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n}\lambda(k,AV)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\langle AVe_{k},e_{k}\rangle+O(1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\langle Ae_{k},e_{k}\rangle\mu(k,V)+O(1).

For
n\geq 1
, let

m(n)=\max\{k\in\mathbb{N}:\ \mu(k,V)>\frac{\|V\|_{1,\infty}}{n}\}.

Using this notation, we write

\sum_{\mu(k,V)>\frac{\|V\|_{1,\infty}}{n}}\langle Ae_{k},e_{k}\rangle\mu(k,V)=\sum_{k=0}^{m(n)}\langle Ae_{k},e_{k}\rangle\mu(k,V).

We have
\mu(k,V)\leq\frac{\|V\|_{1,\infty}}{k+1}
for every
k\geq 0
and, therefore,

m(n)\leq\max\Big\{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}:\ \frac{\|V\|_{1,\infty}}{k+1}>\frac{\|V\|_{1,\infty}}{n}\Big\}=n-2<n.

On the other hand, we have
\mu(k,V)\leq\frac{\|V\|_{1,\infty}}{n}
for all
k>m(n)
. Thus,

\displaystyle\Big|\sum_{k=m(n)+1}^{n}\langle Ae_{k},e_{k}\rangle\mu(k,V)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\|A\|_{\infty}\sum_{k=m(n)+1}^{n}\mu(k,V)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|A\|_{\infty}\|V\|_{1,\infty}}{n}\sum_{k=m(n)+1}^{n}1

\displaystyle=O(1).

Finally, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n}\lambda(k,AV)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\langle Ae_{k},e_{k}\rangle\mu(k,V)+O(1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m(n)}\langle Ae_{k},e_{k}\rangle\mu(k,V)+O(1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu(k,V)>\frac{\|V\|_{1,\infty}}{n}}\langle Ae_{k},e_{k}\rangle\mu(k,V)+O(1).

∎

We now conclude with the proof of Theorem
1.2.7
, a sufficient condition for universal measurability in terms of a
\zeta
-function.
Recall that if
f
is a meromorphic function of a complex variable
z
with a simple pole at
z=0
, then
\mathrm{Res}_{z=0}f(z)
denotes the coefficient of
z^{-1}
in the Laurent
expansion of
f
, or equivalently the value of
zf(z)
at
z=0
.

Theorem
.

Let
0\leq V\in\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}
and let
A\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty}.

Define the
\zeta
-function:

\zeta_{A,V}(z):=\mathrm{Tr}(AV^{1+z}),\quad\Re(z)>0.

If there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\zeta_{A,V}
admits an analytic continuation to the set
\{z\;:\;\Re(z)>-\varepsilon\}\setminus\{0\}

with a simple pole at
0
, then for every normalised trace
\varphi
on
\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}
we have:

\varphi(AV)=\mathrm{Res}_{z=0}\zeta_{A,V}(z).

In particular,
AV
is universally measurable.

Proof.

Assume without loss of generality that
\ker(V)=0
. Select an orthonormal basis
\{e_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}

such that
Ve_{k}=\mu(k,V)e_{k}
.

We define:

b(t):=-t\mathrm{Res}_{w=0}\zeta_{A,V}(w)+\sum_{\mu(k,V)>e^{-t}}\langle Ae_{k},e_{k}\rangle\mu(k,V),\quad t\geq 0.

Since
b
is a linear combination of monotone functions, it is of locally bounded variation. Hence there is a signed Borel measure
b

such that
b([0,x])=b(x)
,
x\geq 0
.

We first prove that
b
satisfies (
5.19
). If
x\geq 0
, then:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{[x,x+1]}b

\displaystyle\leq|\mathrm{Res}_{w=0}\zeta_{A,V}(w)|+\sum_{\mu(k,V)\in(e^{-1-x},e^{-x}]}|\langle Ae_{k},e_{k}\rangle|\mu(k,V)

\displaystyle\leq|\mathrm{Res}_{w=0}\zeta_{A,V}(w)|+2\|A\|_{\infty}e^{-x}\sum_{\mu(k,V)\in(e^{-1-x},e^{-x}]}1

\displaystyle\leq|\mathrm{Res}_{w=0}\zeta_{A,V}(w)|+2\|A\|_{\infty}e^{-x}\sum_{\mu(k,V)\in(e^{-1-x},\infty]}1.

Since
V\in\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty},
we have that

\sum_{\mu(k,V)\in(e^{-1-x},\infty]}1\leq e^{1+x}\|V\|_{1,\infty},\quad x\in\mathbb{R}.

Therefore,

\mathrm{Var}_{[x,x+1]}b\leq|\mathrm{Res}_{w=0}\zeta_{A,V}(w)|+2e\|A\|_{\infty}\|V\|_{1,\infty}.

So
b
indeed satisfies (
5.19
).

Let
\alpha_{k}:=\log(\frac{1}{\mu(k,V)})
and
b_{k}:=\langle Ae_{k},e_{k}\rangle\mu(k,V),
then
the function
b
has a jump discontinuity at the point
\alpha_{k}
of magnitude
b_{k}.
Let
\Re(z)>0
. Using the identity
e^{-\alpha_{k}z}=\mu(k,V)^{z}
, we have:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-zt}db(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}e^{-\alpha_{k}z}\cdot b_{k}-\mathrm{Res}_{w=0}\zeta_{A,V}(w)\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-zt}dt

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}\mu(k,V)^{z}\cdot\langle Ae_{k},e_{k}\rangle\mu(k,V)-\mathrm{Res}_{w=0}\zeta_{A,V}(w)\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-zt}dt

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}\langle AV^{1+z}e_{k},e_{k}\rangle-\mathrm{Res}_{w=0}\zeta_{A,V}(w)\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-zt}dt

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}(AV^{1+z})-\frac{1}{z}\mathrm{Res}_{w=0}\zeta_{A,V}(w).

By assumption, the above right hand side has analytic continuation to the set
\{z\;:\;\Re(z)>-\varepsilon\}
.

Thus, since
b
satisfies (
5.19
) the assumptions of Lemma
5.5.3
are satisfied, and we may then conclude that
b(t)=O(1),
for
t\to\infty
. Thus
by the definition of
b
:,

\sum_{\mu(k,V)>e^{-t}}\langle Ae_{k},e_{k}\rangle\mu(k,V)=t\cdot\mathrm{Res}_{w=0}\zeta_{A,V}(w)+O(1),\quad t\to\infty.

Setting
e^{-t}=\frac{\|V\|_{1,\infty}}{n},
we obtain

\sum_{\mu(k,V)>\frac{\|V\|_{1,\infty}}{n}}\langle Ae_{k},e_{k}\rangle\mu(k,V)=\log(n)\cdot\mathrm{Res}_{w=0}\zeta_{A,V}(w)+O(1),\quad t\to\infty.

By Lemma
5.5.4
, we have

\sum_{k=0}^{n}\lambda(k,AV)=\log(n)\cdot\mathrm{Res}_{w=0}\zeta_{A,V}(w)+O(1),\quad t\to\infty.

The assertion follows now from Theorem
2.1.5
.
∎

5.6.
Proof of Theorem
1.2.5
,
p>2

In this section we complete the proof of Theorem
1.2.5
under the restriction that
p>2
. We require this restriction
in order to directly apply the results of Section
5.4
. We will handle the
p=1
and
p=2
cases separately in the next section.

Lemma 5.6.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
be a spectral triple satisfying Hypothesis
1.2.1
.
Let
0\leq a\in\mathcal{A}.
If
p>2,
then the operators
A=a^{2}
and
B=(1+D^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
satisfy Condition
5.4.1
.

Proof.

Let
D_{0}=F(1+D^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}.
By Lemma
3.1.4
, the spectral triple
(\mathcal{A},H,D_{0})
satisfies Hypothesis
1.2.1
.
Since
|D_{0}|\geq 1
, we have that
\||D_{0}|^{-1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
.

Let us establish Condition
5.4.1
.(
i
).
We have

B^{p}A=|D_{0}|^{-p}a^{2}.

Since
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
is
p
-dimensional, we have that
|D_{0}|^{-p}a\in\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}
, and so
B^{p}A\in\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}
.

Next let us prove Condition
5.4.1
.(
ii
). Let
q>p.
We have

\displaystyle B^{q-2}[B,A]

\displaystyle=|D_{0}|^{2-q}[|D_{0}|^{-1},a^{2}]

\displaystyle=-|D_{0}|^{1-q}\delta_{0}(a^{2})|D_{0}|^{-1}

\displaystyle=-|D_{0}|^{1-q}\delta_{0}(a)a|D_{0}|^{-1}-|D_{0}|^{1-q}a\delta_{0}(a)|D_{0}|^{-1}.

Referring to Lemma
3.1.3
, we have

|D_{0}|^{1-q}\delta_{0}(a)\in\mathcal{L}_{p/(q-1),\infty},\quad|D_{0}|^{1-q}a\in\mathcal{L}_{p/(q-1),\infty},

\delta_{0}(a)|D_{0}|^{-1}\in\mathcal{L}_{p,\infty},\quad a|D_{0}|^{-1}\in\mathcal{L}_{p,\infty}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle B^{q-2}[B,A]

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{L}_{p/(q-1),\infty}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{p,\infty}+\mathcal{L}_{p/(q-1),\infty}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{p,\infty}

So by the Hölder inequality,
B^{q-2}[B,A]\in\mathcal{L}_{p/q,\infty}
. Since
q>p
, it then follows that
B^{q-2}[B,A]\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
.

Now we establish Condition
5.4.1
.(
iii
). We have

A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}}=a|D_{0}|^{-1}a.

Thus,

\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{p,\infty}\leq\|a\|_{\infty}\|a|D_{0}|^{-1}\|_{p,\infty}.

The above right hand side is finite by Lemma
3.1.3
.

Finally we prove Condition
5.4.1
.(
iv
). We recall the notation that
\delta_{0}(a)
denotes the bounded extension of
[|D_{0}|,a]
.
Using (
2.2
), we have:

[B,A^{\frac{1}{2}}]=[|D_{0}|^{-1},a]=-|D_{0}|^{-1}\delta_{0}(a)|D_{0}|^{-1}.

By Theorem 9 in
[
62
]
, we have

|D_{0}|^{-1}\delta_{0}(a)|D_{0}|^{-1}\prec\prec\delta_{0}(a)|D_{0}|^{-2}.

By Lemma
3.1.3
, we have

\delta_{0}(a)|D_{0}|^{-2}\in\mathcal{L}_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}.

Since the norm in the space
\mathcal{L}_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}
is monotone with respect to the Hardy-Littlewood submajorisation (recall that
p>2
), it follows that also

[B,A^{\frac{1}{2}}]=-|D_{0}|^{-1}\delta_{0}(a)|D_{0}|^{-1}\in\mathcal{L}_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}.

∎

Now we may prove Theorem
1.2.5
for the case
p>2
.

Theorem

(
1.2.5
,
p>2
case)
.

Assume
p>2
and let
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
be a spectral triple satisfying Hypothesis
1.2.1
. If
c\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes(p+1)}
is a local Hochschild cycle, then
for every normalised trace
\varphi
on
\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}
we have:

\varphi(\Omega(c)(1+D^{2})^{-\frac{p}{2}})=\mathrm{Ch}(c).

Proof.

By Theorem
1.2.3
, the function

\zeta_{c,D}(z):=\mathrm{Tr}(\Omega(c)(1+D^{2})^{-z/2}),\quad\Re(z)>p

admits an analytic continuation to the set
\{z\;:\;\Re(z)>p-1\}\setminus\{p\}
, and
p
is a simple pole for
\zeta_{c,D}

with residue
p\mathrm{Ch}(c)
.

Let
c=\sum_{j=1}^{m}a^{j}_{0}\otimes a^{j}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{j}_{p}
. We assume that
c
is local, i.e. that there exists
0\leq a\in\mathcal{A}
such that
for all
j
we have
aa^{j}_{0}=a^{j}_{0}
. Equivalently,
(1-a)a^{j}_{0}=0
. So
\mathrm{im}(a^{j}_{0})\subseteq\ker(1-a)
. Since the support projection
\mathrm{supp}(1-a)

is the projection onto the orthogonal complement of the kernel, we have:

\mathrm{supp}(1-a)a^{j}_{0}=0.

By functional calculus,
\mathrm{supp}(1-a)=1-\chi_{\{1\}}(a)
, so moreover we have that
\chi_{\{1\}}(a)a^{j}_{0}=a^{j}_{0}
. Therefore, for all
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
\Re(z)>0
:

a^{2z}a_{0}^{j}=a^{2z}\chi_{\{1\}}(a)a_{0}^{j}=1^{2z}a_{0}^{j}=a_{0}^{j}.

Recall that
\Omega(c)=\sum_{j=0}^{m}\Gamma a_{0}^{j}\partial(a_{1}^{j})\cdots\partial(a_{p}^{j})
. Since
a
commutes with
\Gamma
, we have for all
\Re(z)>0
,

(5.24)

a^{2z}\Omega(c)=\Omega(c).

Let
A=a^{2}
and
B=(1+D^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, as in Lemma
5.6.1
. Then
B^{z}A^{z}=(1+D^{2})^{-z/2}a^{2z}
, and hence

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(\Omega(c)B^{z}A^{z})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}(\Omega(c)(1+D^{2})^{-\frac{z}{2}}a^{2z})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}(a^{2z}\Omega(c)(1+D^{2})^{-\frac{z}{2}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}(\Omega(c)(1+D^{2})^{-\frac{z}{2}}).

By Theorem
1.2.3
, it then follows that
z\mapsto\mathrm{Tr}(\Omega(c)B^{z}A^{z})

admits an analytic continuation to the set
\{\Re(z)>p-1\}\setminus\{p\}
with a simple pole at
z=p

and residue
p\mathrm{Ch}(c)
.

Condition
5.4.1
holds for
A
and
B
by Lemma
5.6.1
. Hence we may apply Theorem
5.4.2
to conclude that

z\to{\rm Tr}\Big(\Omega(c)\Big(B^{z}A^{z}-(A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}})^{z}\Big)\Big)

admits an analytic continuation to the set
\{\Re(z)>p-1\}.

By Lemma
5.6.1
,
A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}}\in\mathcal{L}_{p,\infty}.
Hence, the function (defined
a priori
for
\Re(z)>p
)

z\to{\rm Tr}(\Omega(c)(A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}})^{z})

admits an analytic continuation to the set
\{\Re(z)>p-1\}\setminus\{p\}
, with
z=p
being a simple pole with residue
p\mathrm{Ch}(c).

Consider
V=(A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}})^{p}\in\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}.

It has just been demonstrated that

z\to{\rm Tr}(\Omega(c)V^{z})

admits an analytic continuation to the set
\{\Re(z)>1-\frac{1}{p}\}\setminus\{1\},
with a simple pole at
z=1
and the corresponding residue being
\mathrm{Ch}(c).

By Theorem
1.2.7
, we therefore have

\varphi(\Omega(c)V)=\mathrm{Ch}(c)

for every normalised trace
\varphi
on
\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}.

By Corollary
5.4.11
we have:

V-B^{p}A^{p}=(A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}})^{p}-B^{p}A^{p}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}.

Since
\varphi
vanishes on
\mathcal{L}_{1},
it follows that

\varphi(\Omega(c)B^{p}A^{p})=\varphi(\Omega(c)V)-\varphi(\Omega(c)(V-B^{p}A^{p}))=\varphi(\Omega(c)V)

for every normalised trace
\varphi
on
\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}.
Now using (
5.24
) with
z=p
:

\varphi(\Omega(c)(1+D^{2})^{-\frac{p}{2}})=\varphi(a^{2p}\Omega(c)(1+D^{2})^{-\frac{p}{2}})=\varphi(\Omega(c)B^{p}A^{p})=\mathrm{Ch}(c)

for every normalised trace
\varphi
on
\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}.

∎

5.7.
Proof of Theorem
1.2.5
,
p=1,2

In this final section we complete the proof of Theorem
1.2.5
by dealing with the remaining cases of
p=1
and
p=2
.
We require adjustment for these cases since Theorem
5.4.2
is inapplicable for
p\leq 2
.

Lemma 5.7.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
satisfy Hypothesis
1.2.1
.
Suppose that spectrum of the operator
D
does not intersect the interval
(-1,1).
Then for all
x\in\mathrm{dom}(\delta)
we have an absolute constant
c_{\mathrm{abs}}
such that

\|[|D|^{\frac{1}{3}},x]\|_{1}\leq c_{\mathrm{abs}}\|[|D|,x]\|_{1},

and for all
r\in(1,\infty)
a constant
c_{r}>0
such that

\|[|D|^{\frac{1}{3}},x]\|_{r,\infty}\leq c_{r}\|[|D|,x]\|_{r,\infty}.

These inequalities are understood to be trivially true if the right hand side is infinite.

Proof.

We only prove the first assertion. One can prove the second inequality by an identical argument, with the
\mathcal{L}_{r,\infty}
quasi-norm in place of the
\mathcal{L}_{1}
norm.

Let
f
be a smooth function on
\mathbb{R}
such that
f(t)=|t|^{\frac{1}{3}}
for
|t|>1.
For
\varepsilon>0
, set
f_{\varepsilon}(t)=f(t)e^{-\varepsilon^{2}t^{2}},

t\in\mathbb{R}.
Then,

\displaystyle f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=(f^{\prime}(t)-2\varepsilon^{2}tf(t))e^{-\varepsilon^{2}t^{2}},

\displaystyle f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=(f^{\prime\prime}(t)-4\varepsilon^{2}tf^{\prime}(t)+(4t\varepsilon^{4}-2\varepsilon^{2})f(t))e^{-\varepsilon^{2}t^{2}}

Since for
|t|>1
we have
f^{\prime}(t)=\frac{1}{3}|t|^{-2/3}
, we have that as
\varepsilon\to 0
the
L_{2}
-norm

\|f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R})}
is uniformly bounded. Similarly, since for
|t|>1
,
f^{\prime\prime}(t)=-\frac{2}{9}|t|^{-5/3}
, we
also have that
\|f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(t)\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R})}
is uniformly bounded.

We have that (see e.g.
[
51
, Lemma 7]
):

\|\widehat{f_{\epsilon}^{\prime}}\|_{1}\leq c_{abs}\Big(\|f_{\epsilon}^{\prime}\|_{2}+\|f_{\epsilon}^{\prime\prime}\|_{2}\Big).

and so if
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\|\widehat{f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\|_{1}
is uniformly bounded.

By Lemma
A.2.2
(taken with
s=1
), we have the identity:

[f_{\epsilon}(|D|),x]=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{0}^{1}\hat{f_{\epsilon}^{\prime}}(u)e^{iu(1-v)|D|}\delta(x)e^{iuv|D|}dv\Big)du.

So taking the
\mathcal{L}_{1}
-norm, we conclude from Lemma
2.3.3
that

\displaystyle\Big\|[f_{\epsilon}(|D|),x]\Big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|\hat{f_{\epsilon}^{\prime}}\|_{1}\|\delta(x)\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\mathrm{abs}}\|\delta(x)\|_{1}.

Fix
N>0.
We have

\Big\|\chi_{[0,N]}(|D|)[f_{\epsilon}(|D|),x]\chi_{[0,N]}(|D|)\Big\|_{r,\infty}\leq\frac{c_{abs}r}{r-1}\|\delta(x)\|_{r,\infty}.

Since as
\varepsilon\to 0
, we have that
f_{\varepsilon}
converges uniformly to
f
on the set
[0,N]
, we have that:
As
\epsilon\to 0,
we have

\chi_{[0,N]}(|D|)[f_{\epsilon}(|D|),x]\chi_{[0,N]}(|D|)\to\chi_{[0,N]}(|D|)[|D|^{\frac{1}{3}},x]\chi_{[0,N]}(|D|)

in the operator norm. By the Fatou property of the
\mathcal{L}_{1}
-norm:

\Big\|\chi_{[0,N]}(|D|)\cdot[|D|^{\frac{1}{3}},x]\cdot\chi_{[0,N]}(|D|)\Big\|_{1}\leq c_{\mathrm{abs}}\|\delta(x)\|_{1}.

Since the above inequality is true for arbitrary
N>0,
we may take the limit
N\to\infty
and again using the Fatou property of the
\mathcal{L}_{1}
norm, we arrive at

\Big\|[|D|^{\frac{1}{3}},x]\Big\|_{1}\leq c_{\mathrm{abs}}\|\delta(x)\|_{1}.

∎

As a replacement for Lemma
5.6.1
in the
p=1
case we use the following:

Lemma 5.7.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
be a
1-
dimensional spectral triple satisfying Hypothesis
1.2.1
.
If
0\leq a\in\mathcal{A},
then the operators
A=a^{4}
and
B=(1+D^{2})^{-\frac{1}{6}}
satisfy Condition
5.4.1
with
p=3.

Proof.

This proof is similar to that of Lemma
5.6.1
.

Let
D_{0}=F(1+D^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}.
By Lemma
3.1.4
, the
1-
dimensional spectral triple
(\mathcal{A},H,D_{0})
satisfies Hypothesis
1.2.1
. Since
|D_{0}|\geq 1
,
we have that
\||D_{0}|^{-1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
.

Let us establish Condition
5.4.1
.(
i
). We have

B^{p}A=|D_{0}|^{-1}a^{4}\in\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}

since by assumption
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
is
1
-dimensional.

Next we establish Condition
5.4.1
.(
ii
). Let
q\in(3,4).
Using (
2.2
), we have on
H_{\infty}
:

\displaystyle B^{q-2}[B,A]

\displaystyle=|D_{0}|^{\frac{2-q}{3}}[|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}},a^{4}]

\displaystyle=-|D_{0}|^{\frac{1-q}{3}}[|D_{0}|^{\frac{1}{3}},a^{4}]|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}

\displaystyle=-[|D_{0}|^{\frac{1}{3}},|D_{0}|^{\frac{1-q}{3}}a^{4}|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}].

By Lemma
5.7.1
, we have

\|B^{q-2}[B,A]\|_{1}\leq c_{abs}\|[|D_{0}|,|D_{0}|^{\frac{1-q}{3}}a^{4}|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}]\|_{1}.

Still working on
H_{\infty}
, we also have:

\displaystyle[|D_{0}|,|D_{0}|^{\frac{1-q}{3}}a^{4}|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}]

\displaystyle=|D_{0}|^{\frac{1-q}{3}}\delta_{0}(a^{4})|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}

\displaystyle=|D_{0}|^{\frac{1-q}{3}}\delta_{0}(a^{2})a^{2}|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}+|D_{0}|^{\frac{1-q}{3}}a^{2}\delta_{0}(a^{2})|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}.

Applying Lemma
3.1.3
, we have

[|D_{0}|,|D_{0}|^{\frac{1-q}{3}}a^{4}|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}]\in\mathcal{L}_{\frac{3}{q-1},\infty}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{3,\infty}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\frac{3}{q},\infty}

by the Holder inequality, since
q>3
,
\mathcal{L}_{3/q,\infty}\subset\mathcal{L}_{1}
, and so
B^{q-2}[B,A]\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
.

Now we establish Condition
5.4.1
.(
iii
). We may compute:

A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}}=a^{2}|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}a^{2}.

Thus,

\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{3,\infty}\leq\|a\|_{\infty}^{3}\|a|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}\|_{3,\infty}.

The right hand side is finite by Lemma
3.1.3
.

Finally we verify Condition
5.4.1
.(
iv
). We have:

\displaystyle[B,A^{\frac{1}{2}}]

\displaystyle=[|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}},a^{2}]

\displaystyle=-|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}[|D_{0}|^{\frac{1}{3}},a^{2}]|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}

\displaystyle=-[|D_{0}|^{\frac{1}{3}},|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}a^{2}|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}].

By Lemma
5.7.1
, we have

\|[B,A^{\frac{1}{2}}]\|_{\frac{3}{2},\infty}\leq c_{\mathrm{abs}}\|[|D_{0}|,|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}a^{2}|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}]\|_{\frac{3}{2},\infty}.

Moreover, by the Leibniz rule

\displaystyle[|D_{0}|,|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}a^{2}|D|^{-\frac{1}{3}}]

\displaystyle=|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}\delta_{0}(a^{2})|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}

\displaystyle=|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}\delta_{0}(a)a|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}+|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}a\cdot\delta_{0}(a)|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}.

By Lemma
3.1.3
and the Hölder inequality, we have

[|D_{0}|,|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}a^{2}|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}]\in\mathcal{L}_{3,\infty}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{3,\infty}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\frac{3}{2},\infty}.

∎

For the case
p=2
, we instead use:

Lemma 5.7.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
be a
2-
dimensional spectral triple satisfying Hypothesis
1.2.1
. If
0\leq a\in\mathcal{A},
then the operators
A=a^{4}
and
B=(1+D^{2})^{-\frac{1}{6}}
satisfy Condition
5.4.1
with
p=6.

Proof.

This proof is similar to those of Lemmas
5.6.1
and
5.7.2
.

Let
D_{0}=F(1+D^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}.
By Lemma
3.1.4
, the
2-
dimensional spectral triple
(\mathcal{A},H,D_{0})
satisfies Hypothesis
1.2.1
. By rescaling
D
if necessary,
we may assume without loss of generality that the spectrum of
D_{0}
does not intersect the interval
(-1,1)
.

Let us establish Condition
5.4.1
.(
i
). We have

B^{p}A=|D_{0}|^{-2}a^{4}\in\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}

by Hypothesis
1.2.1
.

Next we establish Condition
5.4.1
.(
ii
). Let
q\in(6,7).
We have

\displaystyle B^{q-2}[B,A]

\displaystyle=|D_{0}|^{\frac{2-q}{3}}[|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}},a^{4}]

\displaystyle=-|D_{0}|^{\frac{1-q}{3}}[|D_{0}|^{\frac{1}{3}},a^{4}]|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}

\displaystyle=-[|D_{0}|^{\frac{1}{3}},|D_{0}|^{\frac{1-q}{3}}a^{4}|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}].

By Lemma
5.7.1
, we have

\|B^{q-2}[B,A]\|_{1}\leq c_{abs}\|[|D_{0}|,|D_{0}|^{\frac{1-q}{3}}a^{4}|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}]\|_{1}.

However,

\displaystyle[|D_{0}|,|D_{0}|^{\frac{1-q}{3}}a^{4}|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}]

\displaystyle=|D_{0}|^{\frac{1-q}{3}}\delta_{0}(a^{4})|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}

\displaystyle=|D_{0}|^{\frac{1-q}{3}}\delta_{0}(a^{2})a^{2}|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}+|D_{0}|^{\frac{1-q}{3}}a^{2}\delta_{0}(a^{2})|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}.

By Lemma
3.1.3
, we have by the Hölder inequality:

[|D_{0}|,|D_{0}|^{\frac{1-q}{3}}a^{4}|D|^{-\frac{1}{3}}]\in\mathcal{L}_{\frac{6}{q-1},\infty}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{6,\infty}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\frac{6}{q},\infty}

Since
q>6
, we have that
\mathcal{L}_{6/q,\infty}\subset\mathcal{L}_{1}
, and so
B^{q-2}[B,A]\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
.

Now we prove Condition
5.4.1
.(
iii
). We have

A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}}=a^{2}|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}a^{2}.

Thus,

\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{6,\infty}\leq\|a\|_{\infty}^{3}\|a|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}\|_{6,\infty}.

The above right hand side is finite by Lemma
3.1.3
.

Finally, let us establish Condition
5.4.1
.(
iv
). We may compute on
H_{\infty}
:

\displaystyle[B,A^{\frac{1}{2}}]

\displaystyle=[|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}},a^{2}]

\displaystyle=-|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}[|D_{0}|^{\frac{1}{3}},a^{2}]|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}

\displaystyle=-[|D_{0}|^{\frac{1}{3}},|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}a^{2}|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}].

Therefore using Lemma
5.7.1
, we have

\|[B,A^{\frac{1}{2}}]\|_{3,\infty}\leq c_{abs}\|[|D_{0}|,|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}a^{2}|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}]\|_{3,\infty}.

Applying the Leibniz rule,

\displaystyle[|D_{0}|,|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}a^{2}|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}]

\displaystyle=|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}\delta_{0}(a^{2})|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}

\displaystyle=|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}\delta_{0}(a)a|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}+|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}a\delta_{0}(a)|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}.

By Lemma
3.1.3
, we then have from the Hölder inequality:

[D_{0},|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}a^{2}|D_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{3}}]\in\mathcal{L}_{6,\infty}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{6,\infty}\subset\mathcal{L}_{3,\infty}.

∎

We may now at last complete the proof of Theorem
1.2.5
.

Theorem
.

Assume
p=1
or
p=2
and let
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
be a spectral triple satisfying Hypothesis
1.2.1
. If
c\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes(p+1)}
is a local Hochschild cycle, then
for every normalised trace
\varphi
on
\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}
we have:

\varphi(\Omega(c)(1+D^{2})^{-\frac{p}{2}})=\mathrm{Ch}(c).

Proof.

By Theorem
1.2.3
, the function

\zeta_{c,D}(z)=\mathrm{Tr}(\Omega(c)(1+D^{2})^{-z/2}),\quad\Re(z)>p

admits an analytic continuation to the set
\{z\;:\;\Re(z)>p-1\}\setminus\{p\}
, and the point
p
is a simple
pole with corresponding residue
p\mathrm{Ch}(c)
.

Let
c=\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{0}^{j}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p}^{j}
. Since
c
is local, we may choose
0\leq a\in\mathcal{A}
such that
aa_{0}^{j}=a_{0}^{j}
for all
j
.

By exactly the same argument as in the
p>2
case, we can show that for all
\Re(z)>0
:

(5.25)

a^{4z}\Omega(c)=\Omega(c).

We let
A=a^{4}
and
B=(1+D^{2})^{-\frac{1}{6}}
as in Lemmas
5.7.2
and
5.7.3
.

We have that:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(\Omega(c)B^{z}A^{z})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}(a^{4z}\Omega(c)(1+D^{2})^{-\frac{z}{6}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}(\Omega(c)(1+D^{2})^{-\frac{z}{6}}),\quad\Re(z)>3p.

We recognise the above function as being precisely
z\mapsto\zeta_{c,D}(z/3)
.
Hence by Theorem
1.2.3
, the function
z\mapsto\mathrm{Tr}(\Omega(c)B^{z}A^{z})
admits an analytic continuation to the set
\{z\;:\;\Re(z)>3(p-1)\}\setminus\{3p\}
,
with a simple pole at
3p
with corresponding residue
3p\mathrm{Ch}(c)
.

Assume now that
p=1.
By Lemma
5.7.2
, Condition
5.4.1
holds for
A
and
B
(with
p=3
). By Theorem
5.4.2
the function

z\to{\rm Tr}\Big(\Omega(c)\Big(B^{z}A^{z}-(A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}})^{z}\Big)\Big)

admits an analytic continuation to the set
\{\Re(z)>2\}.

By Lemma
5.7.2
,
A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}}\in\mathcal{L}_{3,\infty}.
Hence, the function (defined
a priori
for
\Re(z)>3
)

z\to{\rm Tr}(\Omega(c)(A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}})^{z})

admits an analytic continuation to the set
\{\Re(z)>2\}\setminus\{3\}.
with a pole at
z=3
and corresponding residue
3\mathrm{Ch}(c).

Define
V_{1}:=(A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}})^{3}\in\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}.

Then,

\mathrm{Tr}(\Omega(c)(A^{1/2}BA^{1/2})^{z})=\mathrm{Tr}(\Omega(c)V_{1}^{z/3}).

We now know that function

z\mapsto{\rm Tr}(\Omega(c)V_{1}^{z/3})

admits an analytic continuation to the set
\{\Re(z)>2\}\setminus\{3\}
with a simple pole at
3
and corresponding residue
3\mathrm{Ch}(c)
. So by rescaling the argument,
we can equivalently say that the function

z\mapsto\mathrm{Tr}(\Omega(c)V_{1}^{z})

has analytic continuation to the set
\{z\;:\;\Re(z)>2/3\}\setminus\{1\}
with a simple
pole at
1
with corresponding residue
\mathrm{Ch}(c)
.

Thus by Theorem
1.2.7
, for any continuous normalised trace
\varphi
on
\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}
we have

\varphi(\Omega(c)V_{1})=\mathrm{Ch}(c).

Due to Lemma
5.7.2
, we have that
V_{1}-B^{3}A^{3}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
, and since
\varphi
vanishes on
\mathcal{L}_{1}
it follows that

\varphi(\Omega(c)B^{3}A^{3})=\mathrm{Ch}(c).

So

\varphi(a^{12}\Omega(c)(1+D^{2})^{-1/2})=\mathrm{Ch}(c).

By taking
z=3
in (
5.25
), we have that
a^{12}\Omega(c)=\Omega(c)
, this completes the proof in the case
p=1
.

Now assume that
p=2
.
By Lemma
5.7.3
, Condition
5.4.1
holds for
A
and
B
(with
p=6
).
By Theorem
5.4.2
the function

z\to{\rm Tr}\Big(\Omega(c)\Big(B^{z}A^{z}-(A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}})^{z}\Big)\Big)

admits an analytic extension to the set
\{\Re(z)>5\}.

By Lemma
5.7.3
,
A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}}\in\mathcal{L}_{6,\infty}.
Hence, the function (defined
a priori
for
\Re(z)>6
)

z\to{\rm Tr}(\Omega(c)(A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}})^{z})

admits an analytic extension to the set
\{\Re(z)>5\}\setminus\{6\}.
The point
z=6
is a simple pole with corresponding residue
6\mathrm{Ch}(c).
Consider
V_{2}=(A^{\frac{1}{2}}BA^{\frac{1}{2}})^{6}\in\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}.

We have so far shown that the function

z\to{\rm Tr}(\Omega(c)V_{2}^{z})

admits an analytic extension to the set
\{z\;:\;\Re(z)>\frac{5}{6}\}\setminus\{1\}.
The point
z=1
is a simple pole with corresponding residue
\mathrm{Ch}(c)
.

Hence, by Theorem
1.2.7
, for any continuous normalised trace
\varphi
on
\mathcal{L}_{1,\infty}
, we have

\varphi(\Omega(c)V_{2})=\mathrm{Ch}(c).

By Lemma
5.7.3
, the operator
V_{2}-B^{6}A^{6}
is trace class. Thus,

\varphi(\Omega(c)B^{6}A^{6})=\mathrm{Ch}(c).

So
\varphi(a^{12}\Omega(c)(1+D^{2})^{-1})=\mathrm{Ch}(c)
. Since
a^{12}\Omega(c)=\Omega(c)
, this completes the proof for the case
p=2
.
∎

Appendix A
Appendix

A.1.
Properties of the algebra
\mathcal{B}

For this section,
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
is a smooth spectral triple.
Recall from Definition
2.2.7
that
\mathcal{B}
is the
*-
algebra generated by all elements of the form
\delta^{k}(a)
or
\partial(\delta^{k}(a)),

k\geq 0,

a\in\mathcal{A}.

Recall that we define
H_{\infty}:=\bigcap_{k\geq 1}\mathrm{dom}(D^{k})
, and that for all
T\in\mathcal{B}
, we have
T:H_{\infty}\to H_{\infty}
, and for all
k\geq 0
we have
D^{k},|D|^{k}:H_{\infty}\to H_{\infty}
.

The following should be compared with
[
13
, Lemma 6.2]
. See also the discussion following
[
33
, Lemma 10.22]
.

Lemma A.1.1
.

For every
x\in\mathcal{B}
and for every
m\geq 0,
we have the following equalities
of linear (potentially unbounded) operators on
H_{\infty}
:

\displaystyle|D|^{m}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\binom{m}{k}\delta^{m-k}(x)|D|^{k}\text{ and, }

\displaystyle x|D|^{m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^{m-k}\binom{m}{k}|D|^{k}\delta^{m-k}(x).

Proof.

We prove only the first equality as the proof of the second one follows by an identical argument.

This formula can be seen by induction on
m.
Indeed, for
m=1
, this is simply the claim
that since
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathrm{dom}_{\infty}(\delta)
we have an equality of operators on
H_{\infty}
:

|D|x=\delta(x)+x|D|.

Now suppose that the claim is true for
m-1
. Then on
H_{\infty}
we have

\displaystyle|D|^{m}x

\displaystyle=|D|\cdot|D|^{m-1}x

\displaystyle=|D|\cdot\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\binom{m-1}{k}\delta^{m-1-k}(x)|D|^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\binom{m-1}{k}|D|\delta^{m-1-k}(x)\cdot|D|^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\binom{m-1}{k}\delta^{m-1-k}(x)|D|^{k+1}+\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\binom{m-1}{k}\delta^{m-k}(x)|D|^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\binom{m-1}{k-1}\delta^{m-k}(x)|D|^{k}+\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\binom{m-1}{k}\delta^{m-k}(x)|D|^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\binom{m}{k}\delta^{m-k}(x)|D|^{k}.

and so the statement follows for
m
.
∎

Lemma A.1.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
be a smooth spectral triple, and assume that
D
has a spectral gap at
0.
Then for all
x\in\mathcal{B}
and
m\geq 0
we have

(i)

The operators
|D|^{-m}x|D|^{m},|D|^{m}x|D|^{-m}:H_{\infty}\to H_{\infty}
have bounded extension

(ii)

|D|^{1-m}[|D|^{m},x]:H_{\infty}\to H_{\infty}
has bounded extension.

Proof.

By Lemma
A.1.1
, on
H_{\infty}
we have:

\displaystyle|D|^{m}x|D|^{-m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\binom{m}{k}\delta^{m-k}(x)|D|^{k-m},

\displaystyle|D|^{-m}x|D|^{m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^{m-k}\binom{m}{k}|D|^{k-m}\delta^{m-k}(x).

Clearly, the expressions on the right hand side have bounded extension. This proves the first assertion.

By Lemma
A.1.1
, we have

\displaystyle[|D|^{m},x]

\displaystyle=|D|^{m}x-\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^{m-k}\binom{m}{k}|D|^{k}\delta^{m-k}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}(-1)^{m-k-1}\binom{m}{k}|D|^{k}\delta^{m-k}(x).

Therefore,

|D|^{1-m}[|D|^{m},x]=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}(-1)^{m-k-1}\binom{m}{k}|D|^{k+1-m}\delta^{m-k}(x).

Since
x\in\mathrm{dom}_{\infty}(\delta)
, for each
0\leq k\leq m-1
the operator
|D|^{k+1-m}\delta^{m-k}(x)
has bounded extension. Hence,
|D|^{1-m}[|D|^{m},x]

has bounded extension.
∎

Lemma A.1.3
.

Assume that
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
satisfies Hypothesis
1.2.1
. Let
h
be a Borel function on
\mathbb{R}
such that

t\mapsto(1+t^{2})^{\frac{p+1}{2}}h(t),\quad t\in\mathbb{R}.

is bounded.
Then for all
x\in\mathcal{B}
and
s>0
the operator
xh(sD)
is in
\mathcal{L}_{1}
, and:

\|xh(sD)\|_{1}=O(s^{-p}),\quad s\downarrow 0.

Proof.

Let
s>0
. Clearly,

(1+s^{2}D^{2})^{-\frac{p+1}{2}}=|(1-isD)^{-p-1}|.

Setting
\lambda=\frac{1}{s},
we obtain from Hypothesis
1.2.1
that:

\displaystyle\|x(1+s^{2}D^{2})^{-\frac{p+1}{2}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=s^{-p-1}\|x(D+\frac{i}{s})^{-p-1}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=s^{-p-1}\cdot O(s)

\displaystyle=O(s^{-p}),\quad s\downarrow 0.

Since the operator
(1+s^{2}D^{2})^{\frac{p+1}{2}}h(sD)
is bounded, with

\|(1+s^{2}D^{2})^{\frac{p+1}{2}}h(sD)\|_{\infty}\leq\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}(1+t^{2})^{\frac{p+1}{2}}|h(t)|

We can conclude that:

\displaystyle\|xh(sD)\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|x(1+s^{2}D^{2})^{-\frac{p+1}{2}}\|_{1}\|(1+s^{2}D^{2})^{\frac{p+1}{2}}h(sD)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=O(s^{-p}),\quad s\downarrow 0.

∎

We note in particular that the assumption on
h
in Lemma
A.1.3
is satisfied if
h
is a Schwartz function.

Lemma A.1.4
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
satisfy Hypothesis
1.2.1
, assume that
D
has a spectral gap at
0
and let
x\in\mathcal{B}
. For all non-negative integers
m_{1},m_{2}\geq 0
with
m_{1}+m_{2}<p,
we have

\||D|^{-m_{1}}x|D|^{-m_{2}}e^{-s^{2}D^{2}}\|_{1}=O(s^{m_{1}+m_{2}-p}),\quad s\downarrow 0.

Proof.

Suppose first that
m_{1}>0.
Using the triangle inequality:

\displaystyle\||D|^{-m_{1}}x|D|^{-m_{2}}e^{-s^{2}D^{2}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k,l=0}^{\infty}\|\chi_{[2^{k},2^{k+1}]}(|D|)|D|^{-m_{1}}x|D|^{-m_{2}}e^{-s^{2}D^{2}}\chi_{[2^{l},2^{l+1}]}(|D|)\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k,l=0}^{\infty}2^{-km_{1}-lm_{2}}\cdot e^{-2^{2l}s^{2}}\cdot\|\chi_{[2^{k},2^{k+1}]}(|D|)x\chi_{[2^{l},2^{l+1}]}(|D|)\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k,l=0}^{\infty}2^{-km_{1}-lm_{2}}\cdot e^{-2^{2l}s^{2}}\cdot\|\chi_{[0,2^{k+1}]}(|D|)x\chi_{[0,2^{l+1}]}(|D|)\|_{1}.

If
m=\min\{k,l\},
then

\displaystyle\|\chi_{[0,2^{k+1}]}(|D|)x\chi_{[0,2^{l+1}]}(|D|)\Big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\min\Big\{\Big\|x\chi_{[0,2^{m+1}]}(|D|)\Big\|_{1},\Big\|\chi_{[0,2^{m+1}]}(|D|)x\Big\|_{1}\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq e\min\Big\{\Big\|xe^{-2^{-2(m+1)}|D|^{2}}\Big\|_{1},\Big\|x^{*}e^{-2^{-2(m+1)}|D|^{2}}\Big\|_{1}\Big\}

\displaystyle=O(2^{mp}).

Thus,

\||D|^{-m_{1}}x|D|^{-m_{2}}e^{-s^{2}D^{2}}\|_{1}\leq\Big(\sum_{k,l=0}^{\infty}2^{-km_{1}-lm_{2}}\cdot e^{-2^{2l}s^{2}}\cdot 2^{p\cdot\min\{k,l\}}\Big)\cdot O(1).

Since
p-m_{1}>0,
it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k,l\geq 0\\
k\leq l\end{subarray}}2^{-km_{1}-lm_{2}}\cdot e^{-2^{2l}s^{2}}\cdot 2^{p\cdot\min\{k,l\}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k,l\geq 0\\
k\leq l\end{subarray}}2^{(p-m_{1})k}\cdot 2^{-lm_{2}}\cdot e^{-2^{2l}s^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}2^{(p-m_{1})l}\cdot 2^{-lm_{2}}\cdot e^{-2^{2l}s^{2}}.

Now due to our assumption that
m_{1}>0,
it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k,l\geq 0\\
k\geq l\end{subarray}}2^{-km_{1}-lm_{2}}\cdot e^{-2^{2l}s^{2}}\cdot 2^{p\cdot\min\{k,l\}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k,l\geq 0\\
k\geq l\end{subarray}}2^{-km_{1}}\cdot 2^{(p-m_{2})l}\cdot e^{-2^{2l}s^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}2^{-lm_{1}}\cdot 2^{(p-m_{2})l}\cdot e^{-2^{2l}s^{2}}.

Note that for
m>0
, we have

\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}2^{lm}e^{-2^{2l}s^{2}}=O(s^{-m}),\quad s\downarrow 0.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\||D|^{-m_{1}}x|D|^{-m_{2}}e^{-s^{2}D^{2}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}2^{l(p-m_{1}-m_{2})}\cdot e^{-2^{2l}s^{2}}\Big)\cdot O(1)

\displaystyle=O(s^{m_{1}+m_{2}-p}).

This completes the proof for the case
m_{1}>0.

To complete the proof we now deal with the case where
m_{1}=0.
We have

\displaystyle\||D|^{-m_{1}}x|D|^{-m_{2}}e^{-s^{2}D^{2}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\Big\|x|D|^{-m_{2}}e^{-s^{2}D^{2}}\chi_{[2^{l},2^{l+1}]}(|D|)\Big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}2^{-lm_{2}}\cdot e^{-2^{2l}s^{2}}\cdot\Big\|x\chi_{[2^{l},2^{l+1}]}(|D|)\Big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}2^{-lm_{2}}\cdot e^{-2^{2l}s^{2}}\cdot\Big\|x\chi_{[0,2^{l+1}]}(|D|)\Big\|_{1}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\||D|^{-m_{1}}x|D|^{m_{2}}e^{-s^{2}D^{2}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}2^{l(p-m_{2})}\cdot e^{-2^{2l}s^{2}}\Big)\cdot O(1)

\displaystyle=O(s^{m_{2}-p}).

This completes the proof for the case
m_{1}=0.

∎

Lemma A.1.5
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
be a spectral triple satisfying Hypothesis
1.2.1
and assume that
D
has spectral gap at
0.
For all
x\in\mathcal{B}
we have

\|x|D|^{-p-1}(1-e^{-s^{2}D^{2}})\|_{1}=O(s),\quad s\downarrow 0.

Proof.

By the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|x|D|^{-p-1}(1-e^{-s^{2}D^{2}})\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|x|D|^{-p-1}(1-e^{-s^{2}D^{2}})\chi_{(\frac{1}{s},\infty)}(|D|)\|_{1}

(A.1)

\displaystyle\quad+\|x|D|^{-p-1}(1-e^{-s^{2}D^{2}})\chi_{[0,\frac{1}{s}]}(|D|)\|_{1}.

Let us estimate the first summand of (
A.1
). Since for
t>1
we have:

t^{-p-1}(1-e^{-t^{2}})\leq t^{-p-1}\leq 2^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\cdot(t^{2}+1)^{-\frac{p+1}{2}},

it follows that

|D|^{-p-1}(1-e^{-s^{2}D^{2}})\chi_{(\frac{1}{s},\infty)}(|D|)\leq s^{p+1}\cdot 2^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\cdot(1+s^{2}D^{2})^{-\frac{p+1}{2}}.

So we may estimate the first summand by:

\displaystyle\|x|D|^{p-1}(1-e^{-s^{2}D^{2}})\chi_{(\frac{1}{s},\infty)}(|D|)\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\Big\|x(D^{2}+s^{-2})^{-\frac{p+1}{2}}\Big\|_{1}

\displaystyle=2^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\Big\|x(D+\frac{i}{s})^{-p-1}\Big\|_{1}

\displaystyle=O(s)

Where the final step follows from Hypothesis
1.2.1
.(
iii
).

Let us estimate the second summand of (
A.1
). We have

t^{-p-1}(1-e^{-t^{2}})\leq t^{1-p}\leq et^{1-p}\cdot e^{-t^{2}},\quad t\in[0,1],

so it follows that

|D|^{-p-1}(1-e^{-s^{2}D^{2}})\chi_{[0,\frac{1}{s}]}(|D|)\leq es^{p+1}\cdot(s|D|)^{1-p}\cdot e^{-s^{2}D^{2}}.

So, the second summand in (
A.1
) can be estimated by:

\|x|D|^{-p-1}(1-e^{-s^{2}D^{2}})\chi_{[0,\frac{1}{s})]}(|D|)\|_{1}\leq es^{2}\Big\|x|D|^{1-p}e^{-s^{2}D^{2}}\Big\|_{1}.

From Lemma
A.1.4
, this is
O(s)
as
s\downarrow 0
.
∎

Lemma A.1.6
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
be a spectral triple satisfying Hypothesis
1.2.1
and assume that
D
has a spectral gap at
0.
For every
x\in\mathcal{B},
we have

\||D|^{-p-1}x(1-e^{-s^{2}D^{2}})\|_{1}=O(s),\quad s\downarrow 0.

Proof.

On the subspace
H_{\infty}
, we have:

\displaystyle|D|^{-p-1}x

\displaystyle=x|D|^{-p-1}+[|D|^{-p-1},x]

\displaystyle=x|D|^{-p-1}-|D|^{-p-1}[|D|^{p+1},x]|D|^{-p-1}.

By Lemma
A.1.1
, we have (again on
H_{\infty}
):

\displaystyle[|D|^{p+1},x]

\displaystyle=|D|^{p+1}x-x|D|^{p+1}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{k=0}^{p+1}\binom{p+1}{k}\delta^{p+1-k}(x)|D|^{k}\right)-x|D|^{p+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{p}\binom{p+1}{k}\delta^{p+1-k}(x)|D|^{k}.

Thus on
H_{\infty}
:

|D|^{-p-1}x=x|D|^{-p-1}-\sum_{k=0}^{p}\binom{p+1}{k}|D|^{-p-1}\delta^{p+1-k}(x)|D|^{k-p-1}.

Multiplying on the right by
(1-e^{-s^{2}D^{2}})
, on
H_{\infty}
we have:

(A.2)

|D|^{-p-1}x(1-e^{-s^{2}D^{2}})=x|D|^{-p-1}(1-e^{-s^{2}D^{2}})-\sum_{k=0}^{p}\binom{p+1}{k}|D|^{-p-1}\delta^{p+1-k}(x)|D|^{k-p-1}(1-e^{-s^{2}D^{2}})

From Lemma
A.1.5
, we have that
x|D|^{-p-1}(1-e^{-s^{2}D^{2}})
has bounded extension to an operator in
\mathcal{L}_{1}
with norm bounded by
O(s)
.

For
0\leq k\leq p,
we have

\displaystyle\Big\||D|^{-p-1}\delta^{p+1-k}

\displaystyle(x)|D|^{k-p-1}(1-e^{-s^{2}D^{2}})\Big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\Big\||D|^{-p-1}\delta^{p+1-k}(x)\Big\|_{1}\cdot\Big\||D|^{k-p}\Big\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\quad\cdot s\Big\|(s|D|)^{-1}(1-e^{-s^{2}D^{2}})\Big\|_{\infty}.

The first factor here is finite by Remark
3.1.1
. The second factor is finite because
k\leq p.
The third factor is
O(s)
by the functional calculus. Thus,

\||D|^{-p-1}\delta^{p+1-k}(x)|D|^{k-p-1}(1-e^{-s^{2}D^{2}})\|_{1}=O(s),\quad s\downarrow 0.

Hence, each summand in (
A.2
) extends to a trace class operator with norm bounded by
O(s)
,
s\downarrow 0
. By the triangle
inequality, this completes the proof.
∎

A.2.
Integral formulae for commutators

In this section of the appendix, we collect results concerning formulae for commutators with functions of
D
. Many of the results of this section
will be known to the expert reader, but since they are scattered around various sources we provide them here with short and self-contained proofs.

In this section,
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
is a smooth
p
-dimensional spectral triple.

The following is essentially a consequence of the classical Duhamel formula.

Lemma A.2.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
be a smooth spectral triple. If
x\in\mathcal{B}
then for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
:

[e^{it|D|},x]=it\int_{0}^{1}e^{it(1-v)|D|}\delta(x)e^{itv|D|}\,dv.

Here, the integral is understood in the weak operator topology sense.

Proof.

For
n\geq 0
, we define the projection

p_{n}:=\chi_{[0,n]}(|D|).

Since
|D|
is non-negative, as
n\to\infty
the sequence of projections
\{p_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
converges in the strong operator topology to the identity.
We define the functions
\xi
and
\eta
by:

\displaystyle\xi(v)

\displaystyle:=p_{n}\exp(it(1-v)|D|),

\displaystyle\eta(v)

\displaystyle:=x\exp(itv|D|)p_{n},\quad v\in\mathbb{R}.

Since
p_{n}|D|\leq n
, the operator valued functions
\xi
and
\eta
are continuous and differentiable in the uniform norm.

Since
\xi
and
\eta
are continuous and differentiable, we have:

\xi(1)\eta(1)-\xi(0)\eta(0)=\int_{0}^{1}\xi^{\prime}(v)\eta(v)+\xi(v)\eta^{\prime}(v)\,dv.

where since
\xi,

\xi^{\prime},

\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
are continuous, this may be considered as a Bochner integral. Therefore in particular,
this is an integral in the weak operator topology.

We can compute the terms in the integrand as:

\displaystyle\xi^{\prime}(v)\eta(v)

\displaystyle=-itp_{n}\exp(it(1-v)|D|)|D|x\exp(itv|D|)p_{n},

\displaystyle\xi(v)\eta^{\prime}(v)

\displaystyle=itp_{n}\exp(it(1-v)|D|)x|D|\exp(itv|D|)p_{n}.

Thus,

\xi(1)\eta(1)-\xi(0)\eta(0)=-it\int_{0}^{1}p_{n}\exp(it(1-v)|D|)\delta(x)\exp(itv|D|)p_{n}\,dv.

Since the operator
\exp(it(1-v)|D|)\delta(x)\exp(itv|D|)
is a continuous function of
v
(in weak operator topology), the weak operator
topology integral

\int_{0}^{1}\exp(it(1-v)|D|)\delta(x)\exp(itv|D|)\,dv

exists, and we have:

p_{n}\int_{0}^{1}\exp(it(1-v)|D|)\delta(x)\exp(itv|D|)\,dv\cdot p_{n}=\int_{0}^{1}p_{n}\exp(it(1-v)|D|)\delta(x)\exp(itv|D|)p_{n}\,dv.

Therefore:

\xi(1)\eta(1)-\xi(0)\eta(0)=p_{n}\int_{0}^{1}\exp(it(1-v)|D|)\delta(x)\exp(itv|D|)\,dv\cdot p_{n}.

On the other hand, we can compute
\xi(1),\eta(1),\xi(0)
and
\eta(0)
directly:

\displaystyle\xi(1)\eta(1)-\xi(0)\eta(0)

\displaystyle=p_{n}x\exp(it|D|)p_{n}-p_{n}\exp(it|D|)xp_{n}

\displaystyle=p_{n}[x,\exp(it|D|)]p_{n}.

Thus,

p_{n}[\exp(it|D|),x]p_{n}=itp_{n}\int_{0}^{1}\exp(it(1-v)|D|)\delta(x)\exp(itv|D|)\,dv\cdot p_{n}.

Since
n\geq 0
is arbitrary, we may take
n\to\infty
and since
p_{n}
converges in the strong operator topology to the identity
we get the desired equality.
∎

Combining Lemma
A.2.1
with the Fourier inversion theorem yields a formula for
[f(s|D|),x]
for quite general functions
f
. The following formula is well known and appears in many places, for example
[
8
, Theorem 3.2.32]
.

Lemma A.2.2
.

If
\widehat{f},\widehat{f^{\prime}}\in L_{1}(\mathbb{R}),
then for all
x\in\mathcal{B}
, and
s>0
,

[f(s|D|),x]=s\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{f^{\prime}}(u)e^{ius(1-v)|D|}\delta(x)e^{iusv|D|}dv\right)\,du.

Here, the integral is understood in a weak operator topology sense.

Proof.

Indeed, by the Fourier inversion formula, by functional calculus we have a weak operator topology integral:

f(s|D|)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{f}(u)e^{ius|D|}du.

Therefore,

[f(s|D|),x]=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\widehat{f}(u)[e^{ius|D|},x]du.

By Lemma
A.2.1
, we have a weak operator topology integral:

[e^{ius|D|},x]=ius\int_{0}^{1}e^{ius(1-v)|D|}\delta(x)e^{iusv|D|}dv.

Thus,

[f(s|D|),x]=s\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}iu\widehat{f}(u)\Big(\int_{0}^{1}e^{ius(1-v)|D|}\delta(x)e^{iusv|D|}dv\Big)du.

Since
iu\widehat{f}(u)=\widehat{f^{\prime}}(u),
the assertion follows.
∎

Lemma A.2.3
.

If
\widehat{f},\widehat{f^{\prime}},\widehat{f^{\prime\prime}}\in L_{1}(\mathbb{R}),
then for all
x\in\mathcal{B}
and
s>0
we have:

[f(s|D|),x]-sf^{\prime}(s|D|)\delta(x)=-s^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{f^{\prime\prime}}(u)(1-v)e^{ius(1-v)|D|}\delta^{2}(x)e^{iusv|D|}dv\right)du.

[f(s|D|),x]-s\delta(x)f^{\prime}(s|D|)=-s^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{f^{\prime\prime}}(u)(1-v)e^{iusv|D|}\delta^{2}(x)e^{ius(1-v)|D|}dv\right)du.

Here once again the integrals are understood in the weak operator topology.

Proof.

We only prove the first equality as the proof of the second one is similar.

By the Fourier inversion theorem and functional calculus we have a weak operator topology integral representation:

f^{\prime}(s|D|)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{f^{\prime}}(u)e^{ius|D|}du.

So multiplying on the left by the bounded operator
\delta(x)
,

\displaystyle sf^{\prime}(s|D|)\delta(x)

\displaystyle=s\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{f^{\prime}}(u)e^{ius|D|}\delta(x)du

\displaystyle=s\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{f^{\prime}}(u)e^{ius|D|}\delta(x)dv\right)du.

Now representing
[f(s|D|),x]
by the integral representation given by Lemma
A.2.2
, we infer that

\displaystyle[f(s|D|),x]-sf^{\prime}(s|D|)\delta(x)

\displaystyle=s\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{f^{\prime}}(u)\Big(e^{ius(1-v)|D|}\delta(x)e^{iusv|D|}-e^{ius|D|}\delta(x)\Big)dv\Big)du

(A.3)

\displaystyle=s\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{f^{\prime}}(u)\Big(e^{ius(1-v)|D|}[\delta(x),e^{iusv|D|}]\Big)dv\Big)du.

Applying Lemma
A.2.1
to
\delta(x)\in\mathcal{B}
, we have:

(A.4)

[\delta(x),e^{iusv|D|}]=-iusv\int_{0}^{1}e^{iusv(1-w)|D|}\delta^{2}(x)e^{iusvw|D|}dw.

Combining (
A.3
) and (
A.4
), we obtain

(A.5)

[f(s|D|),x]-sf^{\prime}(s|D|)\delta(x)=-s^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{f^{\prime\prime}}(u)ve^{ius(1-vw)|D|}\delta^{2}(x)e^{iusvw|D|}dw\Big)dv\Big)du.

We focus on the inner integral. Performing a linear change of variables,
w_{0}=vw
, we get:

\int_{0}^{1}ve^{ius(1-vw)|D|}\delta^{2}(x)e^{iusvw|D|}\,dw=\int_{0}^{v}e^{ius(1-w_{0})|D|}\delta^{2}(x)e^{iusw_{0}|D|}\,dw_{0},

and therefore:

\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}ve^{ius(1-vw)|D|}\delta^{2}(x)e^{iusvw|D|}\,dwdv=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{v}e^{ius(1-w)|D|}\delta^{2}(x)e^{iusw|D|}\,dwdv.

Since the integrand is continuous, we may apply Fubini’s theorem to interchange the integrals:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}ve^{ius(1-vw)|D|}\delta^{2}(x)e^{iusvw|D|}\,dwdv

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{w}^{1}e^{ius(1-w)|D|}\delta^{2}(x)e^{iusw|D|}\,dvdw

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}(1-w)e^{ius(1-w)|D|}\delta^{2}(x)e^{iusw|D|}\,dw.

So from (
A.5
):

[f(s|D|),x]-sf^{\prime}(s|D|)\delta(x)=-s^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{1}(1-w)e^{ius(1-w)|D|}\delta^{2}(x)e^{iusw|D|}\,dwdu.

∎

A.3.
Hochschild coboundary computations

In this part of the appendix we include some of the lengthy algebraic computations required for Sections
4.3
and
4.4
.
Recall that for a multilinear functional
\theta:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes p}\to\mathbb{C}
the Hochschild coboundary
b\theta:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes(p+1)}\to\mathbb{C}
is defined in terms of the Hochschild boundary
b
by
b\theta(c)=\theta(bc)
.

Explicitly, for an elementary tensor
a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes(p+1)}
we have:

\displaystyle(b\theta)(a_{0}\otimes\cdots a_{p})

\displaystyle=\theta(a_{0}a_{1}\otimes a_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})+\sum_{k=1}^{p-1}(-1)^{k}\theta(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{k}a_{k+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})

\displaystyle\quad+(-1)^{p}\theta(a_{p}a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p-1}).

A.3.1.
Coboundaries in Section
4.3

Let
\mathscr{A}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,p\}
. Let
T:=D^{2-|\mathscr{A}|}|D|^{p+1}e^{-s^{2}D^{2}}.

Following the notation of Definition
4.1.1
, we define
for
a\in\mathcal{A}
,

b_{k}(a):=\begin{cases}\delta(a),\quad k\in\mathscr{A},\\
[F,a],\quad k\notin\mathscr{A}.\end{cases}

Fix
1\leq m\leq p-1
. We introduce a pair of multilinear mappings,
\theta_{s}^{1}
and
\theta_{s}^{2}
, defined on
a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p-1}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes p}
by:

\theta_{s}^{1}(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p-1}):=\mathrm{Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m-2}b_{k}(a_{k})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m-1})\left(\prod_{k=m}^{p-1}b_{k+1}(a_{k})\right)T\right).

and

\theta_{s}^{2}(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p-1}):=\mathrm{Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m-2}b_{k}(a_{k})\right)[F,\delta(a_{m-1})]\left(\prod_{k=m}^{p-1}b_{k+1}(a_{k})\right)T\right).

The mapping that here is denoted
\theta_{s}^{1}
is exactly the multilinear mapping
\theta_{s}
introduced in Lemma
4.3.2
, and similarly
the multilinear mapping
\theta_{s}^{2}
is the multilinear mapping
\theta_{s}
introduced in Lemma
4.3.3
. For
1\leq k<m
we also introduce
X_{k}^{1}
and
X_{k}^{2}
defined by:

\displaystyle X_{k}^{1}

\displaystyle:={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)a_{k}\left(\prod_{l=k}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l+1})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m})\left(\prod_{l=m+1}^{p}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\cdot T\right),

\displaystyle X_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle:={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)a_{k}\left(\prod_{l=k}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l+1})\right)[F,\delta(a_{m})]\left(\prod_{l=m+1}^{p}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\cdot T\right).

Now if
m\leq k\leq p
, we define
Y^{1}_{k}
and
Y^{2}_{k}
by:

\displaystyle Y_{k}^{1}

\displaystyle:={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m-1})\left(\prod_{l=m}^{k-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l})\right)a_{k}\left(\prod_{l=k+1}^{p}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\cdot T\right),

\displaystyle Y_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle:={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)[F,\delta(a_{m-1})]\left(\prod_{l=m}^{k-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l})\right)a_{k}\left(\prod_{l=k+1}^{p}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\cdot T\right).

Lemma A.3.1
.

For
j=1,2
and
a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes(p+1)}
. we have:

\theta^{j}_{s}(a_{0}a_{1}\otimes a_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})=X_{1}^{j}

Proof.

This follows immediately from the definition.
∎

Lemma A.3.2
.

For
j=1,2
,
1\leq k<m-1
and
a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes(p+1)}
we have

\theta_{s}^{j}(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{k}a_{k+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})=X_{k}^{j}+X_{k+1}^{j}.

Proof.

We will only describe the
j=1
case, since the
j=2
case is identical. By the definition of
\theta_{s}^{j},
we have

\displaystyle\theta_{s}^{1}(a_{0}\otimes

\displaystyle a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{k-1}\otimes a_{k}a_{k+1}\otimes a_{k+2}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)b_{k}(a_{k}a_{k+1})\left(\prod_{l=k+1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l+1})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m})\left(\prod_{l=m}^{p-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l+1})\right)\cdot T\right).

Now applying the Leibniz rule to
b_{k}(a_{k}a_{k+1})
,

\displaystyle\theta_{s}^{1}(

\displaystyle a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{k-1}\otimes a_{k}a_{k+1}\otimes a_{k+2}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)a_{k}b_{k}(a_{k+1})\left(\prod_{l=k+1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l+1})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m})\left(\prod_{l=m}^{p-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l+1})\right)\cdot T\right)

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)b_{k}(a_{k})a_{k+1}\left(\prod_{l=k+1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l+1})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m})\left(\prod_{l=m}^{p-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l+1})\right)\cdot T\right)

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)a_{k}\left(\prod_{l=k}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l+1})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m})\left(\prod_{l=m+1}^{p}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)T\right)

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)a_{k+1}\left(\prod_{l=k+1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l+1})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m})\left(\prod_{l=m+1}^{p}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\cdot T\right)

\displaystyle=X_{k}^{1}+X_{k+1}^{1}

as required.
∎

Lemma A.3.3
.

For
m\leq k<p,

j=1,2
and
a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes(p+1)}
we have

\theta_{s}^{j}(a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{k-1}\otimes a_{k}a_{k+1}\otimes a_{k+2}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})=Y_{k}^{j}+Y_{k+1}^{j}.

Proof.

Again we demonstrate only the
j=1
case since the
j=2
case is identical. By definition we have:

\displaystyle\theta_{s}^{1}(

\displaystyle a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{k-1}\otimes a_{k}a_{k+1}\otimes a_{k+2}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m-1})\left(\prod_{l=m}^{k-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l})\right)b_{k+1}(a_{k}a_{k+1})\left(\prod_{l=m+1}^{p-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l+1})\right)\cdot T\right).

Applying the Leibniz rule to
b_{k+1}(a_{k}a_{k+1})
we have:

\displaystyle\theta_{s}^{1}(

\displaystyle a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{k-1}\otimes a_{k}a_{k+1}\otimes a_{k+2}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m-1})\left(\prod_{l=m}^{k-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l})\right)a_{k}b_{k+1}(a_{k+1})\left(\prod_{l=k+1}^{p-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l+1})\right)\cdot T\right)
