\displaystyle\quad+{\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m-1})\left(\prod_{l=m}^{k-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l})\right)b_{k+1}(a_{k})a_{k+1}\left(\prod_{l=k+1}^{p-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l+1})\right)\cdot T\right)

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m-1})\left(\prod_{l=m}^{k-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l}\right)a_{k}\left(\prod_{l=k+1}^{p}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\cdot T\right)

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m-1})\left(\prod_{l=m}^{k}b_{l+1}(a_{l})\right)a_{k+1}\left(\prod_{l=k+2}^{p}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\cdot T\right)

\displaystyle=Y_{k}^{1}+Y_{k+1}^{1}.

∎

We recall also the multilinear maps
\mathcal{W}_{\mathscr{A}}
from Definition
4.1.1
.

Lemma A.3.4
.

Let
c=a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes(p+1)}
. If we assume that
m-1,m\in\mathscr{A}
, then we have:

\displaystyle\theta_{s}^{1}(

\displaystyle a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{m-2}\otimes a_{m-1}a_{m}\otimes a_{m+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})

\displaystyle=X_{m-1}^{1}+Y_{m}^{1}+2{\rm Tr}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathscr{A}}(c)\cdot T).

Now if
\mathscr{B}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,p\}
is such that
|\mathscr{B}|=|\mathscr{A}|
and
\mathscr{A}\Delta\mathscr{B}=\{m-1,m\}
(where
\Delta
denotes the symmetric difference) then

\displaystyle\theta_{s}^{2}(

\displaystyle a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{m-2}\otimes a_{m-1}a_{m}\otimes a_{m+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})

\displaystyle=X_{m-1}^{2}+Y_{m}^{2}+{\rm Tr}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathscr{A}}(c)\cdot T)+{\rm Tr}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathscr{B}}(c)\cdot T).

Proof.

Using the repeated Leibniz rule, we obtain

\displaystyle\delta^{2}(a_{m-1}a_{m})

\displaystyle=\delta^{2}(a_{m-1})a_{m}+2\delta(a_{m-1})\delta(a_{m})+a_{m-1}\delta^{2}(a_{m}),

\displaystyle[F,\delta(a_{m-1}a_{m})]

\displaystyle=[F,\delta(a_{m-1})]a_{m}+[F,a_{m-1}]\delta(a_{m})+\delta(a_{m-1})[F,a_{m}]+a_{m-1}[F,\delta(a_{m})].

Let us focus on proving the assertion relating to
\theta^{1}_{s}
, since the other assertion is identical.

By the definition of
\theta_{s}^{1},
we have

\displaystyle\theta_{s}^{1}(

\displaystyle a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{m-2}\otimes a_{m-1}a_{m}\otimes a_{m+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m-1}a_{m})\left(\prod_{l=m}^{p-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l+1})\right)\cdot T\right)

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m-1}a_{m})\left(\prod_{l=m+1}^{p}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\cdot T\right).

Applying the Leibniz rule to the
\delta^{2}(a_{m-1}a_{m})
term:

\displaystyle\theta_{s}^{1}(

\displaystyle a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{m-2}\otimes a_{m-1}a_{m}\otimes a_{m+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)a_{m-1}\delta^{2}(a_{m})\left(\prod_{l=m+1}^{p}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\cdot T\right)

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)2\delta(a_{m-1})\delta(a_{m})\left(\prod_{l=m+1}^{p}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\cdot T\right)

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m-1})a_{m}\left(\prod_{l=m+1}^{p}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\cdot T\right)

\displaystyle=X_{m-1}+2{\rm Tr}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathscr{A}}(c)\cdot T)+Y_{m}.

∎

Lemma A.3.5
.

For
a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes(p+1)}
, we have

\displaystyle\theta_{s}^{1}(

\displaystyle a_{p}a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes a_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p-1})

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m-1})\left(\prod_{l=m}^{p-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l})\right)[T,a_{p}]\right)+Y^{1}_{p}.

We also have:

\displaystyle\theta_{s}^{2}(

\displaystyle a_{p}a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes a_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p-1})

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)[F,\delta(a_{m-1})]\left(\prod_{l=m}^{p-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l})\right)[T,a_{p}]\right)+Y_{p}^{2}.

Proof.

We prove only the assertion involving
\theta_{s}^{1}
, since one can prove the other result by an identical argument.
Since
\Gamma
commutes with
a_{p}
, we have:

\displaystyle\theta_{s}^{1}(

\displaystyle a_{p}a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes a_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p-1})

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{p}a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m-1})\left(\prod_{l=m}^{p-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l})\right)\cdot T\right)

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(a_{p}\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m-1})\left(\prod_{l=m}^{p-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l})\right)\cdot T\right).

Using the cyclicity of the trace, we have

\displaystyle\theta_{s}^{1}(

\displaystyle a_{p}a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes a_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p-1})

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m-1})\left(\prod_{l=m}^{p-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l})\right)\cdot Ta_{p}\right)

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m-1})\left(\prod_{l=m}^{p-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l})\right)\cdot[T,a_{p}]\right)+Y_{p}^{1}.

∎

Note that for
j=1,2
, the telescopic sum

\displaystyle X_{1}^{j}+

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}(-1)^{k}(X_{k}^{j}+X_{k+1}^{j})+(-1)^{m-1}(X_{m-1}^{j}+Y_{m}^{j})

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=m}^{p-1}(-1)^{k}(Y_{k}^{j}+Y_{k+1}^{j})+(-1)^{p}Y_{p}^{j}.

vanishes.

Therefore, combining Lemmas
A.3.1
,
A.3.2
,
A.3.3
,
A.3.4
and
A.3.5
we have:

\displaystyle(b\theta_{s}^{1})(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})

\displaystyle=2\cdot(-1)^{m-1}\cdot{\rm Tr}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathscr{A}}(c)\cdot T)

\displaystyle\quad+(-1)^{p}\cdot{\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m-1})\left(\prod_{l=m}^{p-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l})\right)\cdot[T,a_{p}]\right).

Similarly, if
\mathscr{B}
is such that
|\mathscr{A}|=|\mathscr{B}|
and
\mathscr{A}\Delta\mathscr{B}=\{m-1,m\}
then:

\displaystyle(b\theta_{s}^{2})(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m-1}\cdot{\rm Tr}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathscr{A}}(c)\cdot T)+(-1)^{m-1}\cdot{\rm Tr}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathscr{B}}(c)\cdot T)

\displaystyle\quad+(-1)^{p}\cdot{\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}b_{l}(a_{l})\right)[F,\delta(a_{m-1})]\left(\prod_{l=m}^{p-1}b_{l+1}(a_{l})\right)\cdot[T,a_{p}]\right).

This completes the computation of the coboundaries for
\theta^{1}_{s}
and
\theta^{2}_{s}
.

A.3.2.
Coboundaries in Section
4.4

Again in this subsection,
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
is a smooth spectral triple where
D
has a spectral gap at
0
. Let
T=Fe^{-s^{2}D^{2}}
. Note that this is different
to
T
in the preceding section.

We define the multilinear mapping
\mathcal{L}_{s}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes p}\to\mathbb{C}
on
a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p-1}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes p}
by:

\mathcal{L}_{s}(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p-1}):={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}[F,a_{k}]\right)\cdot T\right).

We also define the multilinear mapping
\mathcal{K}_{s}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes(p+1)}\to\mathbb{C}
by:

\mathcal{K}_{s}(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p}):={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}[F,a_{k}]\right)\cdot[T,a_{p}]\right).

By definition,
\mathcal{K}_{s}
is exactly the mapping from Theorem
4.4.2
.

For
1\leq m\leq p
, we define
X_{m}
by:

X_{m}:={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m-1}[F,a_{k}]\right)a_{m}\left(\prod_{k=m+1}^{p}[F,a_{k}]\right)T\right).

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{s}(a_{0}a_{1}\otimes a_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}(\Gamma a_{0}a_{1}\prod_{k=2}^{p}[F,a_{k}]\cdot T)

\displaystyle=X_{1}.

Applying the Leibniz rule to
[F,a_{m-1}a_{m}]
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{s}(

\displaystyle a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{m-2}\otimes a_{m-1}a_{m}\otimes a_{m+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m-2}[F,a_{k}]\right)[F,a_{m-1}a_{m}]\left(\prod_{k=m+1}^{p}[F,a_{k}]\right)\cdot T\right)

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m-2}[F,a_{k}]\right)a_{m-1}\left(\prod_{k=m}^{p}[F,a_{k}]\right)\cdot T\right)

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m-1}[F,a_{k}]\right)a_{m}\left(\prod_{k=m+1}^{p}[F,a_{k}]\right)\cdot T\right)

\displaystyle=X_{m}+X_{m+1}.

Finally,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{s}(a_{p}a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p-1})

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}(\Gamma a_{p}a_{0}\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}[F,a_{k}]\cdot T)

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}(\Gamma a_{0}\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}[F,a_{k}]\cdot Ta_{p})

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}(\Gamma a_{0}\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}[F,a_{k}]\cdot[T,a_{p}])+{\rm Tr}(\Gamma a_{0}\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}[F,a_{k}]\cdot a_{p}T)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{K}_{s}(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})+X_{p}.

Thus,

\displaystyle(b\mathcal{L}_{s})(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{K}_{s}(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})

\displaystyle\quad+X_{1}+\Big(\sum_{m=1}^{p-1}(-1)^{m}(X_{m}+X_{m+1})\Big)+(-1)^{p}X_{p}.

The latter sum telescopes and indeed vanishes, so it follows that
b\mathcal{L}_{s}=\mathcal{K}_{s}.

A.3.3.
Coboundaries in Section
4.5

In this section,
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
is a smooth spectral triple where
D
has a spectral gap at
0
, and
T:=|D|^{2-p}e^{-s^{2}D^{2}}
.
We define the multilinear mapping
\theta_{s}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes p}\to\mathbb{C}
on
a_{0}\otimes\cdots a_{p-1}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes p}
by:

\theta_{s}(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p-1})={\rm Tr}\left(\left(\prod_{k=0}^{p-1}\partial(a_{k})\right)T\right).

For
0\leq k\leq p
we also define:

X_{k}={\rm Tr}\left(\left(\prod_{l=0}^{k-1}\partial(a_{l})\right)a_{k}\left(\prod_{l=k+1}^{p}\partial(a_{l})\right)T\right).

So in particular,

X_{0}=\mathrm{Tr}\left(a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{p}\partial(a_{l})\right)T\right).

Applying the Leibniz rule to
\partial(a_{k}a_{a+1})
we get:

\displaystyle\theta_{s}(

\displaystyle a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{k-1}\otimes a_{k}a_{k+1}\otimes a_{k+2}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\left(\prod_{l=0}^{k-1}\partial(a_{l})\right)\partial(a_{k}a_{k+1})\left(\prod_{l=k+2}^{p}\partial(a_{l})\right)\cdot T\right)

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\left(\prod_{l=0}^{k-1}\partial(a_{l})\right)a_{k}\left(\prod_{l=k+1}^{p}\partial(a_{l})\right)\cdot T\right)

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm Tr}\left(\left(\prod_{l=0}^{k}\partial(a_{l})\right)a_{k+1}\left(\prod_{l=k+2}^{p}\partial(a_{l})\right)\cdot T\right)

\displaystyle=X_{k}+X_{k+1}.

We also have

\displaystyle\theta_{s}(

\displaystyle a_{p}a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p-1})

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left(\left(\prod_{k=0}^{p-1}\partial(a_{k})\right)\cdot Ta_{p}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm Tr}\left(a_{0}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{p-1}\partial(a_{k})\right)\cdot T\partial(a_{p})\right)

\displaystyle=X_{p}+{\rm Tr}\left(\left(\prod_{k=0}^{p-1}\partial(a_{k})\right)\cdot[T,a_{p}]\right)

\displaystyle\quad+X_{0}+{\rm Tr}\left(a_{0}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}\partial(a_{k})\right)\cdot[T,\partial(a_{p})]\right).

If we assume that
p
is even, then:

\displaystyle(b\theta_{s})(

\displaystyle a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}(-1)^{k}(X_{k}+X_{k+1})\right)+(X_{p}+X_{0})

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm Tr}\left(\left(\prod_{k=0}^{p-1}\partial(a_{k})\right)\cdot[T,a_{p}]\right)+{\rm Tr}\left(a_{0}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}\partial(a_{k})\right)\cdot[T,\partial(a_{p})]\right)

\displaystyle=2X_{0}+{\rm Tr}\left(a_{0}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}\partial(a_{k})\right)[T,\partial a_{p}]\right)+{\rm Tr}\left(\left(\prod_{k=0}^{p-1}\partial(a_{k})\right)[T,a_{p}]\right)

\displaystyle=2{\rm Tr}\left(a_{0}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{p}\partial(a_{k})\right)\cdot T\right)+{\rm Tr}\left(a_{0}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}\partial(a_{k})\right)[T,\partial a_{p}]\right)

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm Tr}\left(\left(\prod_{k=0}^{p-1}\partial(a_{k})\right)[T,a_{p}]\right).

This completes the computation of the coboundaries.

A.4.
Technical estimates for Section 4.4.1

For this section,
(\mathcal{A},H,D)
is assumed to be a spectral triple satisfying Hypothesis
1.2.1
and we
assume that
D
has a spectral gap at
0
.

The results of this section are very similar to that of Lemma
4.1.3
. However additional technicalities make the proofs more involved and therefore are included here in the appendix.
We make use of the mappings
b_{k}
from Definition
4.1.1
, defined in terms of
m\geq 1
and a subset
\mathscr{B}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,m\}
on
a\in\mathcal{A}

b_{k}(a):=\begin{cases}\delta(a),\quad k\in\mathscr{B},\\
[F,a],\quad k\notin\mathscr{B}.\end{cases}

Lemma A.4.1
.

Let
m\geq 1
and Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then for all
\mathscr{B}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,m\}
the operator on
H_{\infty}
given by:

|D|^{-n}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m}b_{k}(a_{k})\right)|D|^{n+m-|\mathscr{B}|}

has bounded extension, where
b_{k}
is defined in terms of
\mathscr{B}
.

Proof.

We prove the assertion by induction on
m.
If
m=1
then there are two possible cases,
\mathscr{B}=\emptyset
and
\mathscr{B}=\{1\}
.

If
\mathscr{B}=\emptyset
, then on
H_{\infty}
we have:

\displaystyle|D|^{-n}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m}b_{k}(a_{k})\right)|D|^{n+m-|\mathscr{B}|}

\displaystyle=|D|^{-n}[F,a_{1}]|D|^{n+1}

\displaystyle=|D|^{-n}L(a_{1})|D|^{n}

\displaystyle=|D|^{-n}\partial(a_{1})|D|^{n}-F|D|^{-n}\delta(a_{1})|D|^{n}.

By Lemma
A.1.2
, the operators
|D|^{-n}\partial(a_{1})|D|^{n}
and
|D|^{-n}\delta(a_{1})|D|^{n}
have bounded extension, so this proves
the
\mathscr{B}=\emptyset
case. On the other hand, if
\mathscr{B}=\{1\},
then on
H_{\infty}
:

|D|^{-n}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m}b_{k}(a_{k})\right)|D|^{n+m-|\mathscr{B}|}=|D|^{-n}\delta(a_{1})|D|^{n}.

Again by Lemma
A.1.2
, the operator
|D|^{-n}\delta(a_{1})|D|^{n}
has bounded extension. This completes the proof of the
\mathcal{B}=\{1\}
case.

Now assume that
m>1
and the assertion is true for
m-1
. Define
\mathscr{C}:=\mathscr{B}\setminus\{m\}.
and let
n_{1}=n+(m-1)-|\mathscr{C}|
. Then on
H_{\infty}
:

\displaystyle|D|^{-n}

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m}b_{k}(a_{k})\right)|D|^{n+m-|\mathscr{B}|}

(A.6)

\displaystyle=\left(|D|^{-n}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m-1}b_{k}(a_{k})\right)|D|^{n+(m-1)-|\mathscr{C}|}\right)\left(|D|^{-n_{1}}b_{m}(a_{m})|D|^{n_{1}+1-|\mathscr{B}\cap\{m\}|}\right).

By the inductive assumption, the first factor has bounded extension.

If
m\in\mathscr{B},
then the second factor in (
A.6
) is:

|D|^{-n_{1}}\delta(a_{m})|D|^{n_{1}}

which has bounded extension by Lemma
A.1.2
.(
i
). On the other hand, if
m\notin\mathscr{B},
then the second factor in (
A.6
) is

|D|^{-n_{1}}F(a_{m})|D|^{n_{1}+1}=|D|^{-n_{1}}(\partial(a_{m})-F\delta(a_{m}))|D|^{n_{1}}

which also has bounded extension by Lemma
A.1.2
.(
i
). In either case, the second factor of (
A.6
) has bounded extension. So the assertion is proved
for
m
, completing the proof by induction.
∎

Lemma A.4.2
.

Let
\mathscr{A}\subseteq\{1,\cdots,p\}
Assume that there is
m>1
be such that
m-1,m\in\mathscr{A}.

The operator on
H_{\infty}
given by:

\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m-2}b_{k}(a_{k})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m-1})\left(\prod_{k=m}^{p-1}b_{k+1}(a_{k})\right)|D|^{p-|\mathscr{A}|}

has bounded extension.

Proof.

Let
n_{1}=|\{1,\cdots,m-2\}\setminus\mathscr{A}|
and
n_{2}=|\{m+1,\cdots,p\}\setminus\mathscr{A}|
so that immediately
n_{1}\leq m-2-|\mathscr{A}|
and
n_{2}\leq p-m-|\mathscr{A}|
. On
H_{\infty}
we have

\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m-2}b_{k}(a_{k})\right)\delta^{2}(a_{m-1})\left(\prod_{k=m}^{p-1}b_{k+1}(a_{k})\right)|D|^{p-|\mathscr{A}|}=\mathrm{I}\cdot\mathrm{II}\cdot\mathrm{III}.

Here,

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}

\displaystyle=\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m-2}b_{k}(a_{k})\right)|D|^{n_{1}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{II}

\displaystyle=|D|^{-n_{1}}\delta^{2}(a_{m-1})|D|^{n_{1}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{III}

\displaystyle=|D|^{-n_{1}}\left(\prod_{k=m}^{p-1}b_{k+1}(a_{k})\right)|D|^{n_{1}+n_{2}}.

The operators
\mathrm{I}
and
\mathrm{III}
have bounded extension by Lemma
A.4.1
. On the other hand,
\mathrm{II}
has bounded extension due to Lemma
A.1.2
.
∎

Lemma A.4.3
.

Let
\mathscr{A}\subset\{1,\cdots,p\}
and assume that there is
m>1
be such that
m-1\in\mathscr{A}
and
m\notin\mathscr{A}.
The operator on
H_{\infty}
given by

\displaystyle\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m-2}b_{k}(a_{k})\right)[F,\delta(a_{m-1})]\left(\prod_{k=m}^{p-1}b_{k+1}(a_{k})\right)|D|^{p-|\mathscr{A}|}

has bounded extension.

Proof.

Let
n_{1}=|\{1,\cdots,m-2\}\setminus\mathscr{A}|
and
n_{2}=|\{m+1,\cdots,p\}\setminus\mathscr{A}|,
so that we immediately have
n_{1}\leq m-2-|\mathscr{A}|
and
n_{2}\leq p-m-|\mathscr{A}|
as in Lemma
A.4.2
. We have

\Gamma a_{0}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m-2}b_{k}(a_{k})\right)[F,\delta(a_{m-1})]\left(\prod_{k=m}^{p-1}b_{k+1}(a_{k})\right)|D|^{p-|\mathscr{A}|}=\Gamma a_{0}\cdot\mathrm{I}\cdot\mathrm{II}\cdot\mathrm{III}.

Here,

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m-2}b_{k}(a_{k})\right)|D|^{n_{1}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{II}

\displaystyle=|D|^{-n_{1}}[F,\delta(a_{m-1})]|D|^{1+n_{1}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{III}

\displaystyle=|D|^{-n_{1}-1}\left(\prod_{k=m}^{p-1}b_{k+1}(a_{k})\right)|D|^{1+n_{1}+n_{2}}.

The operators
\mathrm{I}
and
\mathrm{III}
have bounded extension by Lemma
A.4.1
. On the other hand,

\mathrm{II}=|D|^{-n_{1}}(\partial(\delta(a_{m-1}))-F\delta^{2}(a_{m-1}))|D|^{n_{1}}

which has bounded extension by Lemma
A.1.2
.(
i
).
∎

Lemma A.4.4
.

For every
m\geq 1,
the operator on
H_{\infty}
given by

\Big(\prod_{k=0}^{m}[F,a_{k}]\Big)|D|^{m+1}

has bounded extension.

Proof.

We prove the assertion by induction on
m.
For
m=0,
on
H_{\infty}
we have

[F,a_{0}]|D|=\partial(a_{0})-F\delta(a_{0})

which has has bounded extension.

Now let
m>1
and assume that the assertion is true for
m-1
. On
H_{\infty}
we write

\Big(\prod_{k=0}^{m}[F,a_{k}]\Big)|D|^{m+1}=\Big(\Big(\prod_{k=0}^{m-1}[F,a_{k}]\Big)|D|^{m}\Big)\cdot\Big(|D|^{-m}[F,a_{m}]|D|^{m+1}\Big).

The first factor has bounded extension by the induction assumption. The second factor is

|D|^{-m}L(a_{m})|D|^{m}=|D|^{-m}\partial(a_{m})|D|^{m}-F\cdot|D|^{-m}\delta(a_{m})|D|^{m}

which has bounded extension by Lemma
A.1.2
.(
i
). Hence, the assertion holds for
m
, completing the inductive proof.
∎

A.5.
Subkhankulov’s computation

The following assertion is identical to
[
61
, Lemma 2.1.1]
. However to the best of our knowledge there is no published proof in English,
and
[
61
]
is not easily accessible. For the convenience of the reader we include a proof here.

Proposition A.5.1
.

For all
u\in(0,1)
and
v\in\mathbb{R},
we have

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{(1-t^{2})^{2}}{u+it}e^{(u+it)v}dt=(1+u^{2})^{2}\chi_{[0,\infty]}(v)+e^{uv}\cdot\min\{1,v^{-2}\}\cdot O(1).

Proof.

We will deal separately with the
v\geq 0
and
v<0
cases. First, assume that
v\geq 0
.

Let
\gamma_{0}
be a smooth curve in
\mathbb{C}
without self-intersections such that

(1)

\gamma_{0}
starts at
i
and ends at
-i.

(2)

\gamma_{0}
lies in the half-plane
\{\Re(z)\leq 0\},

(3)

The distance between
\gamma_{0}
and the interval
[-1,0]
is greater than or equal to
1
,

(4)

the length of
\gamma_{0}
is at most
10.

(5)

\gamma_{0}
is contained in the disc
\{z\;:\;|z|\leq 10\}
.

Let
\gamma_{1}
be the interval starting at
-i
and ending at
i
and let the contour
\gamma
be the concatenation of
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}.

Define

f(z):=\frac{(1+z^{2})^{2}}{u+z},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{-u\}.

So that by definition:

(A.7)

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{(1-t^{2})^{2}}{u+it}e^{(u+it)v}\,dt=\frac{1}{2\pi i}e^{uv}\int_{\gamma_{1}}f(z)e^{zv}\,dz.

Since
z\mapsto e^{zv}
is entire, the function
z\to f(z)e^{zv}
is holomorphic in the set
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{-u\}
and has a simple pole at at
z=-u

with corresponding residue
(1+u^{2})^{2}e^{-uv}.
By construction,
the point
-u
is in the interior of the curve
\gamma
and so
by the Cauchy integral formula we have:

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\gamma}f(z)e^{zv}dz=(1+u^{2})^{2}e^{-uv}.

Since
\gamma=\gamma_{0}\cup\gamma_{1}
:

(A.8)

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\gamma_{0}}f(z)e^{zv}dz+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\gamma_{1}}f(z)e^{zv}dz=(1+u^{2})^{2}e^{-uv}.

By definition
\gamma_{0}
has length at most
10
, so by the triangle inequality we have the bound:

\left|\int_{\gamma_{0}}f(z)e^{zv}dz\right|\leq 10\sup_{z\in\gamma_{0}}|f(z)||e^{zv}|.

Since
\gamma_{0}
is contained in the half-plane
\{z\;:\;\Re(z)\leq 0\}
we also have that
\sup_{z\in\gamma_{0}}|e^{zv}|\leq 1

and therefore:

\left|\int_{\gamma_{0}}f(z)e^{zv}dz\right|\leq 10\sup_{z\in\gamma_{0}}|f(z)|.

For
z\in\gamma
we have that
|z|\leq 10
and
|u+z|\geq 1
so it follows that

\sup_{z\in\gamma_{0}}|f(z)|\leq(1+10^{2})^{2}\leq 10^{5}.

Therefore, we have

(A.9)

\int_{\gamma_{0}}f(z)e^{zv}dz=O(1).

Using integration by parts twice and taking into account that
f(\pm i)=f^{\prime}(\pm i)=0,
we obtain

\int_{\gamma_{0}}f(z)e^{zv}dz=v^{-2}\int_{\gamma_{0}}f^{\prime\prime}(z)e^{vz}dz.

Thus,

v^{2}\left|\int_{\gamma_{0}}f(z)e^{zv}dz\right|\leq 10\sup_{z\in\gamma_{0}}|f^{\prime\prime}(z)|.

We may compute
f^{\prime\prime}(z)
directly as:

f^{\prime\prime}(z)=(4+12z)\frac{1}{u+z}-8z(1+z^{2})\frac{1}{(u+z)^{2}}+(1+z^{2})^{2}\frac{2}{(u+z)^{3}}.

Since
|z|\leq 10
and
|u+z|\geq 1
for every
z\in\gamma_{0},
it follows that

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\gamma_{0}}|f^{\prime\prime}(z)|

\displaystyle\leq(4+12\cdot 10)+8\cdot 10\cdot(1+10^{2})+2\cdot(1+10^{2})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 10^{5}.

Therefore, we have:

(A.10)

\int_{\gamma_{0}}f(z)e^{zv}dz=O(v^{-2}),

Hence,

(A.11)

\int_{\gamma_{0}}f(z)e^{zv}dz=\min\{1,v^{-2}\}\cdot O(1).

Combining (
A.8
) and (
A.11
), we obtain

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\gamma_{1}}f(z)e^{zv}dz=(1+u^{2})^{2}e^{-uv}+\min\{1,v^{-2}\}\cdot O(1).

So using (
A.7
) and (
A.8
):

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{(1-t^{2})^{2}}{u+it}e^{(u+it)v}dt=(1+u^{2})^{2}+e^{uv}\cdot\min\{1,v^{-2}\}\cdot O(1).

This completes the proof of the
v\geq 0
.

Now assume that
v<0
.
This proof is similar, but instead we consider a contour in the half plane
\{z\;:\;\Re(z)\geq 0\}
.
Let
\gamma_{2}
be a smooth curve without self-intersections such that

(1)

\gamma_{2}
starts at
-i
and ends at
i.

(2)

\gamma_{2}
lies in the half-plane
\{\Re(z)\geq 0\}.

(3)

the distance between
\gamma_{2}
and
[-1,0]
is greater than or equal to
1
.

(4)

the length of
\gamma_{2}
is at most
10.

(5)

\gamma_{2}
is contained in the disc
\{z\;:\;|z|\leq 10\}
.

As in the
v\geq 0
case,
\gamma_{1}
denotes the interval joining
-i
and
i
, and now write
\gamma^{\prime}
for the concatenation of
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
.

Since
f
is holomorphic in the half-plane
\Re(z)\geq 0
, we have:

\int_{\gamma^{\prime}}f(z)e^{zv}dz=0.

Since
\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma_{1}\cup\gamma_{2}
we have:

(A.12)

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\gamma_{1}}f(z)e^{zv}dz+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\gamma_{2}}f(z)e^{zv}dz=0.

Since by definition
\gamma_{2}
has length at most
10
, and we are assuming
v<0
we have:,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\gamma_{2}}f(z)e^{zv}dz\right|

\displaystyle\leq 10\sup_{z\in\gamma_{2}}|f(z)e^{zv}|

\displaystyle\leq 10\sup_{z\in\gamma_{2}}|f(z)|

\displaystyle\leq 10(1+10^{2})^{2}.

\displaystyle=O(1).

Using integration by parts and taking into account once again that
f(\pm i)=f^{\prime}(\pm i)=0,
we obtain in also in the
v<0
case that:

\int_{\gamma_{2}}f(z)e^{zv}dz=v^{-2}\int_{\gamma_{2}}f^{\prime\prime}(z)e^{vz}dz.

Thus,

\displaystyle v^{2}\left|\int_{\gamma_{2}}f(z)e^{zv}dz\right|

\displaystyle\leq 10\sup_{z\in\gamma_{2}}|f^{\prime\prime}(z)||e^{zv}|

\displaystyle\leq 10^{6}

\displaystyle=O(1).

Therefore,

(A.13)

\int_{\gamma_{2}}f(z)e^{zv}dz=\min\{1,v^{-2}\}\cdot O(1).

Combining (
A.12
) and (
A.13
), we obtain

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\gamma_{1}}f(z)e^{zv}dz=\min\{1,v^{-2}\}\cdot O(1).

Hence, by (
A.7
) we conclude the proof for the
v<0
case.
∎
